ii 
 
 
PREFACE 
 
 I, the Chairman of the Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Home Affairs, 
having been authorized by the Committee to submit the report on its behalf, do hereby present this Two 
Hundred Fifty Seventh Report on Demands for Grants (2026-27) relating to the Ministry of Home 
Affairs (MHA). 
2. Under Rule 272 of the Rules of Procedure and Conduct of Business in the Council of States, the 
Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Home Affairs is mandated to consider the 
Demands for Grants of the related Ministries and make report thereon. In pursuance thereof, the 
Committee in its meetings held on 16th and 17th February, 2026 heard the Home Secretary and other 
officers and considered the Demands for Grants (2026-27) of the Ministry of Home Affairs.  
3. The Committee in its sitting held on 16th March, 2026 considered and adopted the Report. 
4. The Committee while making its observations/recommendations has mainly relied upon the 
following documents:- 
 (i) Speech of Finance Minister on 1st February, 2026 while presenting the Union Budget 2026-
27; 
 (ii) Detailed Demands for Grants of the Ministry of Home Affairs for the year 2026-27; 
(iii) Output, Outcome, Monitoring framework of the Ministry 2026-27; 
(iv) Detailed Explanatory Notes on Demands for Grants of the Ministry of Home Affairs for the 
year 2026-27; 
(v) Written replies furnished by the Ministry to the Questionnaires sent to them by the 
Secretariat;  
(vi) Presentations made by the Home Secretary and other concerned officers;  
(vii) Written clarifications furnished by the Ministry, on the points/issues raised by the Members 
during the deliberations of the Committee; and 
(viii) Verbatim transcripts of the meetings of the Committee held on 16th and 17th February, 
2026. 
 
5. For facility of reference and convenience, observations and recommendations of the Committee 
have been printed in bold letters in the body of the Report. 
                                
16th March, 2026

CHAPTER-I 
OVERVIEW 
 
1.1  CONSTITUTIONAL AND FUNCTIONAL MANDATE 
 
1.1.1 The Ministry of Home Affairs (MHA) is entrust ed with the responsibility of ensuring 
internal security, border management, administratio n of Union Territories, Centre–State 
relations, disaster management, citizenship matters  and related subjects. The Ministry plays a 
pivotal role in maintaining public order, strengthe ning national integration and ensuring 
coordinated security responses across the country. 
 
1.1.2  Though 'Public Order' and 'Police' are the r esponsibilities of States as per the Seventh 
Schedule to the Constitution of India, Article 355 of the Constitution enjoins the Union to protect 
every State against external aggression and interna l disturbance and to ensure that the 
Government of every State is carried on in accordan ce with the provisions of the Constitution. 
While discharging its duties, the Ministry of Home Affairs continuously monitors the internal 
security situation, issues appropriate advisories, shares intelligence inputs, extends manpower 
and financial support, guidance and expertise to th e State Governments for maintenance of 
security, peace and harmony without encroaching upon the constitutional rights of the States. 
 
1.1.3  The Detailed Demands for Grants (2026-27) re flect the Ministry’s wide-ranging mandate 
covering Secretariat administration, Modernisation of Prisons and Other Administrative Services 
including Civil Defence and Fire Services, Miscella neous General Services and Police 
Infrastructure, among others. 
 
1.2 GOALS AND OBJECTIVES 
 
1.2.1 The MHA administers and implements a wide ran ge of schemes and programmes. In 
brief, the goals and objectives of the Ministry for the year (2026-27) are as follows: 
 
(i) Continuous monitoring of  internal security situati on and issuance of timely 
advisories; 
(ii) Consolidation of gains towards achieving a Naxal-fr ee Bharat through 
coordinated security and development initiatives in  Left Wing Extremism 
affected areas; 
(iii) Implementation of a zero-tolerance policy against t errorism including 
strengthening of counter-terrorism architecture and  intelligence coordination 
mechanisms;  
(iv) Adoption of a Whole-of-Government approach to disma ntle narco-terror 
networks and combat drug trafficking; 
(v) Strengthening border management through integrated border infrastructure, 
coastal security, surveillance technologies and Vibrant Villages Programme; 

2 
 
(vi) Modernisation of the criminal justice system throug h implementation of new 
criminal laws and digitization to ensure a transpar ent, time-bound and victim-
centric criminal justice system; 
(vii) Further development of the Inter-Operable Criminal Justice System (ICJS) for 
seamless data exchange among police, courts, prison s and forensic 
institutions; 
(viii) Strengthening forensic science and investigation in frastructure under the 
National Forensic Infrastructure Enhancement initia tives, including 
modernization of Central Forensic Science Laboratories (CFSLs); 
(ix) Further expansion of evidence-based investigation s ystems, and enhancement 
of training and skill-development programmes for police personnel; 
(x) Enhancing cyber security architecture and strengthe ning institutional 
mechanisms to address emerging digital and financial cyber threats; 
(xi) Strengthening disaster preparedness and response ca pacity through capacity 
building, early warning systems and coordinated dis aster management 
frameworks; 
(xii) Modernization, capacity building and welfare measur es for Central Armed 
Police Forces (CAPFs), including provision of secur ity, administrative and 
residential infrastructure; 
(xiii) Ensuring timely reimbursement to States under Secur ity Related Expenditure 
(SRE) and providing Special Infrastructure Support to LWE affected States; 
(xiv) Conduct of Census 2027 and related preparatory acti vities, including 
technological upgradation for census operations; 
(xv) Operationalisation and strengthening of Bhartiya Bh asha Anubhag and 
promotion of Official Languages to support translat ion services and linguistic 
diversity; 
(xvi) Strengthening cooperative federalism through the In ter-State Council and 
Zonal Councils; 
(xvii) Implementing initiatives for women's safety and emp owerment to ensure a 
secure environment for women; 
(xviii) Fostering international cooperation on security iss ues, including counter-
terrorism and transnational crime; 
(xix) Supporting prison reforms and modernization of pris ons, including schemes 
for support to poor prisoners; 
(xx) Streamlining immigration processes through technolo gy-driven systems 
including the Immigration, Visa, Foreigners Registr ation and Tracking 
(IVFRT) and related platforms;  
(xxi) Timely disbursement of funds to freedom fighters an d their eligible 
dependents; 
(xxii) Supporting holistic development of islands and bord er areas in Union 
Territories to enhance local governance and infrastructure; and 
(xxiii) Fostering community engagement programs to promote peace, harmony and 
social cohesion; 

3 
 
1.3 ORGANIZATIONAL SET UP OF THE MHA 
 
1.3.1 The Ministry functions through multiple Divis ions. The list of existing Divisions of the 
Ministry of Home Affairs is given below:-

(iv) Central Reserve Police Force;

(vi) National Security Guard; and

to the Ministry and functioning under its administrative control:- 
 
(i)

CHAPTER-II 
 
OVERALL ASSESSMENT OF DEMANDS FOR GRANTS (2026-27) OF 
THE MHA 
 
2.1 OVERVIEW 
 
2.1.1 There are eleven Demands for Grants which are  administered by the Ministry of Home 
Affairs (MHA). The detailed Demands for Grants of t he Ministry of Home Affairs are divided 
into three volumes: Vol. I, Vol. II-A and Vol. II-B. The Detailed Demands for Grants (Volume-I) 
contains three Demands (Nos. 49-51), namely the Min istry of Home Affairs (Demand No. 49); 
the Cabinet (Demand No. 50); and the Police (Demand No. 51). 
 
2.1.2. The Detailed Demands for Grants (Volumes-II- A and II-B) relate to the five Union 
Territories (without Legislature), namely Andaman &  Nicobar Islands (Demand No. 52), 
Chandigarh (Demand No. 53), Dadra & Nagar Haveli an d Daman & Diu (Demand No. 54), 
Ladakh (Demand No. 55) and Lakshadweep (Demand No. 56) and  three Union Territories (with 
Legislature), namely Delhi (Demand No. 57- Transfer s to Delhi), Jammu & Kashmir (Demand 
No.58- Transfers to Jammu & Kashmir) and Puducherry  (Demand No. 59- Transfers to 
Puducherry). 
 
2.1.3 The provisions for the Union Territories (wit hout Legislature) form part of the Demands 
of the Ministry of Home Affairs. However, various p rogrammes/schemes are the subject matter 
of different Ministries, which are responsible for scrutinizing, sanctioning and implementing the 
schemes/projects falling under their respective pur view. Provisions for the Union Territories 
(with a Legislature) cover Assistance to the Union Territories of Puducherry, Jammu & Kashmir 
and NCT of Delhi, in the form of the Grants and Loa ns. In respect of these three Grants, the 
Ministry of Home Affairs releases the Grants on a q uarterly basis and has a limited role in 
scrutinizing, sanctioning and implementation of the schemes/projects. 
 
2.2 OVERALL BUDGETARY ALLOCATION (2026-27) OF THE M INISTRY OF 
HOME AFFAIRS 
 
2.2.1 The Committee was informed by the Ministry th at the total allocation granted for all the 
eleven Demands for Grants of the Ministry of Home A ffairs in BE 2026-27 is ₹2,61,339.60 
crores. This represents an increase of 4.72% over t he Revised Estimates for 2025-26 
(₹2,49,551.69 crores); and an increase of 8.30% ove r the Budget Estimates for 2025-26 
(₹2,41,293.45 crores). In 2026-27, the allocation t o the Ministry of Home Affairs constitutes 
approximately 5% of the total Union Budget. The det ails of allocation of all the eleven Demands 
for the Financial Year 2025-26 (BE & RE) and Financ ial Year 2026-27 (BE) are given in Table 
2.1 below:

2.2.2 It can be seen from Table 2.1 above that the allocation made under Demand No. 49- 
Ministry of Home Affairs for BE 2026-27 is ₹10,387. 35 crores as against ₹4,557.19 crores in BE 
2025-26, reflecting a substantial increase. Similar ly, the allocation under Demand No. 51 – 
Police for BE 2026-27 is ₹1,74,873.63 crores as com pared to ₹1,61,445.37 crores in BE 2025-
26, indicating a moderate enhancement in provision. 
 
2.2.3 The Committee notes these variations across m ajor Demands and observes that while the 
increase under Demand No. 49 is substantial, largel y driven by Census-related provision, the 
increase under Demand No. 51 is moderate and reflec ts incremental strengthening of internal 
security expenditure. 

7 
 
2.2.4 The Committee further notes that the allocati on made under Demand Nos. 52-59 
pertaining to the Union Territories for BE 2026-27 is ₹70,861.50 crores, which constitutes 
approximately 27.11% of the total allocation of ₹2, 61,339.60 crores to the Ministry in BE 2026-
27. As compared to BE 2025-26, the overall allocati on for Union Territories shows an increase. 
Out of this, the combined allocation for the five U nion Territories without Legislature for BE 
2026-27 is ₹22,705.32 crores, while the three Union  Territories with Legislature have been 
allocated ₹48,156.18 crores. The Committee notes th at the allocations to both categories of 
Union Territories have registered an upward movemen t during the year, indicating continued 
budgetary support for their administrative and developmental requirements. 
2.2.5 For BE 2026-27, the total allocation for the Ministry of Home Affairs stands at 
₹2,61,339.60 crores, reflecting an increase over bo th the Budget Estimates and Revised 
Estimates of the previous year. The total allocatio n granted for the Ministry at BE stage for the 
past three years i.e., 2023-24, 2024-25 and 2025-26  was ₹1,96,034.94 crores, ₹2,19,643.31 
crores and ₹2,41,293.45 crores respectively. The Co mmittee observes that there has been a 
consistent upward trajectory in the budgetary allocation to the Ministry over the years. 
2.2.6 The Committee views the enhanced allocation a s reflective of the expanding operational 
responsibilities of the Ministry and emphasises the  need for sustained financial support in the 
areas of internal security, border management, disa ster preparedness and Union Territory 
administration.

representing 86.86% of the RE. The Revenue componen t has recorded utilisation of 87.62%, 
while Capital expenditure stands at 79.86% of RE. F or the combined Demands (49 and 51), 
against the total Revised Estimate of ₹1,70,684.80 crores, an expenditure of ₹1,46,624.45 crores 
has been incurred, which constitutes 85.90% of the RE. The Committee notes that while 
utilisation under Demand No. 51 remains robust, par ticularly under the Revenue head, utilisation 
under Demand No. 49 is comparatively lower and may require closer monitoring to avoid 
bunching of expenditure towards the end of the financial year.

Schemes/Projects and Other Central Sector Expenditu re. During the presentations before the 
Committee, the officials of the Ministry assured th e Committee that the required funds may be 
provided to various heads according to the utilizat ions pattern at the RE Stage.  The Committee 
notes the assurance of the Ministry and emphasises that availability of funds should not become 
a constraining factor for critical activities of the Ministry, including Census operations. 
 
2.4.3 The total allocation under Demand No. 49 at B E 2026-27 has increased to ₹10,387.35 
crores as against ₹7,480.15 crores at RE 2025-26. T he increase is primarily attributable to 
provision of ₹6,000.00 crores for Census, Survey an d Statistics/Registrar General of India under 
Other Central Sector Expenditure head. The Committe e notes that Census operations constitute 
the principal driver of the increase under this Demand during the year under consideration.

of fund under Development Grant to Manipur head at the BE stage of 2026-27. 
 
2.4.5  During the course of examination, the Ministry indi cated that additional requirements, if 
any, would be addressed at the Revised Estimates st age based on expenditure trends. The 
Committee emphasises that availability of funds sho uld not become a constraining factor for 
critical activities, particularly Census operations  and disaster management preparedness. The

Committee expects that any emerging requirement dur ing the course of the year may be 
promptly addressed at the Revised Estimates stage.

2.5.2 The gross allocation under Demand No. 51 at B E 2026-27 is ₹1,74,873.63 crores, with 
net allocation of ₹1,73,802.53 crores, as against n et allocation of ₹1,62,283.39 crores at RE 
2025-26. The projected requirement for BE 2026-27 w as ₹2,00,494.90 crores (Net), whereas the 
final allocation stands at ₹1,73,802.53 crores, ref lecting moderation at the stage of budget 
finalisation. The final allocation is approximately 13.31% lower than the projected requirement. 
 
2.5.3 Under Establishment Expenditure of the Centre , allocation at BE 2026-27 stands at 
₹1,49,836.06 crores as against ₹1,41,690.40 crores at RE 2025-26. The increase is primarily 
under Central Armed Police Forces, Intelligence Bur eau, Delhi Police and Jammu & Kashmir 
Police, reflecting enhanced operational and administrative requirements. 
 
2.5.4 Under Central Sector Schemes/Projects, alloca tion at BE 2026-27 is ₹13,824.66 crores as 
against ₹11,348.74 crores at RE 2025-26. Major comp onents include Border Infrastructure and 
Management, Police Infrastructure, Schemes for Safe ty of Women, Modernisation Plan-IV for 
CAPFs and Inter Operable Criminal Justice System. 
 
2.5.5 Under Other Central Sector Expenditure, alloc ation at BE 2026-27 stands at ₹5,725.41 
crores as against ₹5,498.89 crores at RE 2025-26. U nder Centrally Sponsored Schemes, 
allocation at BE 2026-27 is ₹4,411.40 crores as against ₹3,745.36 crores at RE 2025-26. 
 
Observation/Recommendation 
 
2.5.6 The Committee notes that while overall alloca tion under Demand No. 51 has 
increased in BE 2026-27, the allocation is lower th an the projected requirement under 
several modernization and infrastructure-related he ads. The Committee is of the view that 
sustained moderation from projected levels, particu larly under Police Infrastructure, 
Border Infrastructure and technology upgradation, m ay affect the pace of capacity 
enhancement. 
 
2.5.7 The Committee, therefore, recommends that the  Ministry undertake periodic 
review of expenditure under critical heads and take  up additional requirements, if 
warranted, at the Revised Estimates stage so that p riority programmes relating to internal 
security, border management and modernization are n ot adversely impacted due to 
shortage of funds. 

17 
 
 
2.5.8 The Committee further emphasises the importan ce of effective financial 
management, timely utilisation of funds and avoidan ce of disproportionate expenditure in 
the last quarter of the financial year to ensure optimal outcomes from the allocations made.

Observation/ Recommendation 
 
2.6.7 The total expenditure incurred by the Union T erritories with Legislature (Delhi, 
Jammu & Kashmir and Puducherry) against the Revised  Estimates of FY 2025-26 stands 
at ₹42,882.63 crores, which is 93.02% of the RE all ocation as on 31 st  January, 2026. The 
Committee notes that utilisation levels among these  Union Territories are comparatively 
higher. The Committee stressed upon the need for ti mely release of funds and effective 
coordination between the Ministry and the respective UT administrations to ensure smooth 
execution of schemes. The Committee observes that a llocations for Union Territories 
continue to constitute a significant portion of the  Ministry’s overall budget. Given the 
developmental requirements of the UTs, particularly  in respect of physical infrastructure, 
social sector services and administrative strengthe ning, adequate provision under the 
Capital Head assumes importance. Particular attenti on may be accorded to UTs with 
fragile ecological and geographic conditions, where  infrastructure development entails 
additional financial implications. 
 
2.6.8 The Committee recommends that the Ministry cl osely monitor capital expenditure 
in the Union Territories and address any emerging s hortfall at the Revised Estimates stage 
so that ongoing projects and developmental initiati ves are not adversely affected. The 
Committee further recommends that capital-intensive  projects in geographically sensitive 
UTs may be prioritised to avoid implementation delays. 
 
2.6.9 The Committee notes that while the overall bu dgetary allocation to the Ministry has 
increased during the year under review, moderation between projected requirements and 
final Budget Estimates is evident across certain he ads. The Committee is of the view that 
internal security, Census operations, disaster mana gement preparedness and 
infrastructure modernisation constitute critical na tional priorities and require sustained 
and timely financial support. The Committee expects  that additional requirements, based 
on utilisation trends, may be considered at the Rev ised Estimates stage so that priority 
programmes are not adversely affected.

20 
 
 
CHAPTER III 
 
DEMAND NO. 49 - MINISTRY OF HOME AFFAIRS 
 
3.1 OVERVIEW 
3.1.1 Ministry of Home Affairs contains provisions mainly for various administrative functions 
in respect of MHA Secretariat, Office of Registrar General and Census Commissioner of India, 
various offices functioning under Department of Off icial Language, Zonal Councils, National 
Disaster Response Force, National Disaster Manageme nt Authority (NDMA), Director General 
of Fire Service, Civil Defence & Home Guards, Natio nal Human Rights Commission, National 
Fire Services College and Departmental Accounting Organisation of MHA.  
3.1.2 Apart from the above, provisions have been ma de for schemes like Relief & 
Rehabilitation for repatriates and migrants, Pensio n and other benefits to Freedom Fighters, 
Helicopter subsidies for North-East, Jammu & Kashmi r, Ladakh and Himachal Pradesh and 
various schemes of Disaster Management and Establis hment of Bhartiya Bhasha Anubhag 
(BBA).  
3.2 OFFICIAL LANGUAGE 
3.2.1 The allocations under Official Language inclu de provisions for Central Hindi Training 
Institute (CHTI), Central Translation Bureau (CTB),  Technical Cell, Regional Implementation 
Offices (RIOs) and other schemes / programmes being  implemented by the Department of 
Official Language. 
3.2.2  The Committee sought to know about the measu rable outcomes proposed to be achieved 
under the head “Official Languages” during 2026–27 and the specific targets fixed for 
translation, capacity building and inspection activ ities. The Ministry informed that the following 
outputs have been proposed for the year:  
(i)
 Under translation activities, the Indian Languages Section proposes to translate 
approximately 20,000 letters into various regional languages, while the Central 
Translation Bureau has fixed a target of translating 40,000 pages during 2026–27. 
(ii) Under capacity building, the Department aims to exp and the Hindi Shabd Sindhu 
repository from 7 lakh to 7.5 lakh words and prepar e a Medical Shabd Sindhu 
dictionary in 15 Indian languages. It has also prop osed to impart Hindi training to 
25,000 personnel through the Central Hindi Training Institute and provide training to 
1,175 personnel through the Central Translation Bureau. 
(iii) With regard to inspections, the Parliamentary  Committee on Official Language has 
proposed to inspect approximately 7,000 offices. Fu rther, approximately 852 
offices/banks/undertakings are to be inspected by t he Regional Implementation 
Offices. 

21 
 
3.2.3 The Committee further sought to know the addi tional measures and initiatives 
undertaken for promotion of Official Language Hindi  apart from quarterly and annual assessment 
reports. The Ministry informed that the Department of Official Language has undertaken the 
following initiatives: 
(i) Development and upgradation of the Artificial Intel ligence-based translation software 
“Bharati - Bahubhashi Anuvada Sarthi” (earlier Kant hastha), integrated with e-Office 
and expanded to include 15 Indian languages, with p rovision for instant translation 
facility. 
(ii) Establishment of the Indian Languages Section to fa cilitate multilingual 
correspondence between the Central Government and S tate Governments in their 
respective official languages through a universal translation system. 
(iii) Continuous expansion of the fully digital Hindi-to- Hindi dictionary “Hindi Shabd 
Sindhu”, which presently contains over seven lakh words across diverse domains. 
(iv) Inspired by the Hon’ble Prime Minister’s “Panch Pra n,” the Department has organised 
a series of All India Official Language Conferences  and Regional Official Language 
Conferences to strengthen implementation of the Off icial Language Policy and confer 
Rajbhasha awards for exemplary performance. 
(v) Conduct of Technical Conferences for capacity enhan cement of officers of the Central 
Secretariat Official Language Service. 
(vi) Functioning of over 550 Town Official Language Impl ementation Committees 
(TOLICs) across the country, including five oversea s TOLICs, to promote the use of 
Hindi in official work. 
(vii) Periodic review of implementation through the Centr al Hindi Committee, chaired by 
the Hon’ble Prime Minister, and additionally, under  the chairmanship of the Secretary, 
Department of Official Language, a meeting of the C entral Official Language 
Implementation Committee (COLIC) is organized every year.  
(viii) Launch of the e-Saral Hindi Phrasebook containing m ore than 14,000 official-use 
sentences including terms drawn from domains such a s administration, agriculture, 
banking and finance, healthcare, industry, information technology, law, and tourism. 
3.2.4 From the information provided by the MHA, it may be seen that while the allocation in  
RE of 2025-26 was ₹ 97.06 crores, in BE 2026-27 an allocation of ₹ 98.16 crores has been made 
under the head ‘Official language’. 
Observations/Recommendations 
3.2.5  The Committee notes the measurable targets fixed by  the Department of Official 
Language for 2026–27, including translation of 20,0 00 letters and 40,000 pages, imparting 
Hindi training to 25,000 personnel, training of 1,1 75 officials by the Central Translation 

22 
 
Bureau, expansion of the Hindi Shabd Sindhu reposit ory to 7.5 lakh words, preparation of 
a Medical Shabd Sindhu dictionary in 15 Indian lang uages, and inspection of 
approximately 852 offices/banks/undertakings by Reg ional Implementation Offices. The 
Committee appreciates the outcome-oriented approach  reflected in the fixing of 
quantifiable physical targets and expects effective  implementation of the proposed targets. 
Further, the Ministry may explore expansion of domain-specific terminologies, particularly 
in emerging sectors such as digital governance, art ificial intelligence, cyber security and 
health sciences, so as to strengthen the practical usability of Hindi in technical and 
administrative communication. 
3.2.6 The Committee further notes the technological  interventions undertaken by the 
Department, particularly the upgradation of the AI- based translation platform “Bharati – 
Bahubhashi Anuvada Sarthi”, its integration with e- Office, and the establishment of the 
Indian Languages Section to facilitate multilingual correspondence between the Centre and 
States. The Committee is of the view that while the se initiatives are commendable, the 
Ministry may consider instituting a robust performa nce-monitoring framework to assess 
the quality, timeliness and actual utilisation of t ranslated content across Ministries and 
Departments. The Committee also recommends that per iodic impact assessment studies 
may be undertaken to evaluate the effectiveness of Hindi training programmes and the 
functional usage of trained personnel in official work.  
3.3 NATIONAL DISASTER RESPONSE FORCE (NDRF) 
 
3.3.1 The Committee, in its meeting while discussin g the Demands for Grants (2026–27) of the 
Ministry of Home Affairs, appreciated the efforts o f the National Disaster Response Force 
(NDRF) in rescue and rehabilitation during disasters, noting that its personnel have demonstrated 
exceptional courage and professionalism in safeguar ding lives and property across a wide range 
of calamities.  
3.3.2 The Committee was apprised that during the pe riod 2023–2025, NDRF conducted 3,421 
operations deploying 5,256 teams. During these oper ations, 12,528 persons were rescued and 
1,55,387 persons were evacuated. Further, 2,146 dea d bodies were retrieved, 4,322 livestock 
were saved and medical assistance was provided to 3 7,545 persons. In 2025 alone, the Force 
conducted 1,489 operations across 27 States/UTs aff ected by floods, cloudbursts, landslides, 
cyclones and other disasters. The NDRF also underto ok international humanitarian assistance 
under “Operation Bramha” during the Myanmar earthquake. 
3.3.3 The Committee was further informed that NDRF presently has 16 operational battalions. 
No new battalion has been raised during the last th ree years. The authorised strength of the 
NDRF is 18,581 personnel against a posted strength of 14,837 personnel. 
3.3.4 With regard to infrastructure augmentation, t he Ministry informed that ₹140.00 crore has 
been proposed for infrastructure development in FY 2026–27 at nine Regional Response Centres 
(RRCs) located at Agartala, Lucknow, Gorakhpur, Gan gtok, Nurpur, Hyderabad, Ernakulam, 

23 
 
Port Blair and Gandhinagar, along with works at bat talion locations and ₹3.62 crore for 
acquisition of additional land at Dehradun, Chamoli and Uttarkashi. 
3.3.5 The total allocation for NDRF in BE 2026–27 i s ₹2102.11 crores, out of which ₹213.77 
crores (approximately 10.17%) has been earmarked fo r capital expenditure. The capital 
component includes ₹96.35 crore for Buildings & Str uctures, ₹11.00 crores for Motor Vehicles, 
₹90.00 crores for Machinery & Equipment, ₹7.50 cror es for ICT infrastructure, ₹5.00 crore sfor 
Furniture & Fixtures, ₹3.62 crores for Land acquisi tion and ₹0.30 crores for other fixed assets. 
An amount of ₹3.00 crores has been earmarked for training of personnel. 
Observations/Recommendations 
3.3.6 
 The Committee notes the significant increase in ope rational commitments of the 
NDRF, as reflected in the rising number of operatio ns conducted during the last three 
years. The Committee appreciates the role played by  the Force in responding to disasters 
across multiple States/UTs and its participation in  international humanitarian assistance 
operations. However, the Committee observes that de spite increasing operational 
responsibilities, no additional battalion has been raised during the last three years and the 
posted strength (14,837) remains considerably below  the authorised strength (18,581). The 
Committee recommends that the Ministry may undertak e a comprehensive assessment of 
manpower gaps and expedite measures to fill vacanci es so as to ensure optimal operational 
readiness. The Terms of deputation may be revised t o incentivize more personnel from 
Central armed police Forces (CAPFs) to join the NDRF.  
3.3.7 The Committee appreciates the critical role o f the NDRF in disaster response and 
emphasizes the need for adequate funding to address  its demands, particularly for 
additional personnel. The shortage of funds for suc h critical schemes can adversely impact 
the functioning of the NDRF. The Committee recommen ds that the MHA may approach 
the Ministry of Finance at the Revised Estimates (R E) stage to secure the additional funds, 
if required, ensuring that operational efficiency a nd preparedness are not compromised 
during emergencies. 
3.3.8 The Committee further observes that only abou t 10% of the total allocation in BE 
2026–27 has been earmarked for capital expenditure.  In view of the increasing frequency 
and intensity of natural disasters, the Committee r ecommends that greater emphasis may 
be placed on strengthening capital assets, advanced  rescue equipment, ICT-enabled 
response systems and regional infrastructure to red uce response time and enhance disaster 
preparedness.

North Eastern Region, Union Territory of Ladakh and  Union Territory of Jammu & Kashmir.

operational requirements. The allocation under the Scheme is as under: 
 Union Territory of Jammu & Kashmir – ₹6.00 crore 
 Union Territory of Ladakh – ₹6.00 crore 
 North Eastern Region – ₹94.19 crore 
3.6.2 The Committee further sought details of activ ities undertaken and utilisation of funds 
under Helicopter Services during 2025–26. The Minis try informed that helicopter services are 
utilised for medical and emergency evacuation from remote and border areas, disaster response 
operations, delivery of essential supplies, critica l care transportation and airlifting of people 
during road closures due to snowfall. The Ministry,  further, stated that the subsidy component 
ensures reliable and uninterrupted connectivity to remote and border regions. It was also 
informed that a new sector, namely Jammu–Mendhar an d Mendhar–Jammu in the Jammu 
Division, was taken up during FY 2025–26.

3.7  INFRASTRUCTURE  FOR DISASTER  MANAGEMENT 
3.7.1 The Ministry informed that the Scheme provide s for construction of Office and 
Residential Buildings for the National Disaster Res ponse Force (NDRF), acquisition of land and 
construction of premises for the National Institute  of Disaster Management (NIDM), and 
development of specialised infrastructure for rapid  intervention during disasters. It was further 
stated that ₹81.29 crore was utilised during FY 202 4–25 against the Budget Estimate (BE) of 
₹152.00 crore, while ₹45.30 crore was utilised duri ng FY 2025–26 (upto 31.12.2025) against the 
BE of ₹100.00 crore. The Ministry explained that th e variation between RE 2025–26 and BE 
2025–26 was mainly due to reduction in demand for Building and Structures by NDRF. 
3.7.2 The Ministry, further, informed that the Sche me is a Central Sector Scheme implemented 
by the National Disaster Response Force (NDRF) and is currently under revision for 
continuation beyond March, 2026, with most ongoing projects stated to be at the verge of 
completion. The allocation ₹100.00 Cr for BE 2026–2 7 is intended for infrastructure 
development at nine Regional Response Centres (RRCs ) located at Agartala, Lucknow, 
Gorakhpur, Gangtok, Nurpur, Hyderabad, Ernakulam, P ort Blair and Gandhinagar, along with 
certain works at Battalion locations and other RRCs  of NDRF, with the timeline for completion 
fixed as March, 2027. The Ministry further clarifie d that strengthening of early warning systems 
does not form part of the Scheme of Infrastructure for Disaster Management. 
3.7.3 The Committee noted that allocation under “Ot her Disaster Management Schemes” 
remains minimal in BE 2026–27 and sought clarificat ion regarding continuation of earlier 
programmes. The Ministry informed that out of 17 su b-schemes approved upto March, 2026, 10 
sub-schemes are proposed to be continued beyond Mar ch, 2026 while the remaining are 
proposed to be closed, and that the Scheme is being  considered for continuation with 
amalgamation of existing and proposed new sub-schemes. With regard to Information, Education 
and Communication (IEC), capacity-building and trai ning initiatives, the Ministry stated that 
well-established institutional mechanisms exist at the National, State and District levels to 
strengthen preparedness and ensure prompt and effec tive disaster response. The Committee also 
noted following major initiatives:- 
(i) Implementation of the Common Alerting Protocol (CAP )-based Integrated Alert System 
for dissemination of geo-targeted early warnings th rough SMS, TV, Radio, Indian 
Railways, Coastal Sirens and other mediums across all 36 States/UTs. 
(ii) Launch of the Extension of Emergency Response Suppo rt System (ERSS) through single 
emergency number “112” for disaster-related emergencies. 

28 
 
(iii) Implementation of the Aapda Mitra Scheme under whic h 1,00,000 community volunteers 
have been trained in 350 multi-hazard disaster-prone districts across all States/UTs. 
Observation/Recommendation 
3.7.4 The Committee notes that some sub-schemes und er Disaster Management Schemes 
are presently under revision for continuation beyon d March, 2026. The Committee 
recommends that the Ministry may finalise well in a dvance, a comprehensive roadmap 
clearly outlining the objectives, scope, financial outlay and measurable outcomes of the 
revised Scheme so as to avoid any implementation ga ps and ensure seamless continuity of 
infrastructure development. The Committee further r ecommends that an outcome-based 
evaluation framework may be incorporated in the rev ised Scheme to assess the impact of 
infrastructure augmentation on response time, opera tional readiness and disaster 
resilience. In this regard, the Committee is of the  view that lessons learnt from community-
based initiatives such as the Aapda Mitra Scheme ma y be systematically documented and 
integrated into future planning, particularly in st rengthening last-mile preparedness, 
community participation and coordination between trained volunteers and response forces. 
The revised scheme must aim to develop pan-India mu lti-hazard response capability. India 
needs a strong disaster data infrastructure to coll ect, collate and analyse information to 
help policy makers make informed decisions. 
3.8 CENSUS, SURVEY AND  STATISTICS/REGISTRAR  GENER AL OF INDIA 
3.8.1.  The Ministry informed the Committee that Population  Census is the largest 
administrative exercise in the country and is condu cted under the provisions of the Census Act, 
1948 and the Census Rules, 1990. India has conducte d regular decennial censuses since 1872, 
and Census 2027 will be the 16th Census in the cont inuous series and the 8th since 
Independence. The Census scheduled for 2021 was pos tponed due to the COVID-19 pandemic. 
The Government has now notified its intent to condu ct Census 2027 vide Gazette Notification 
dated 16.06.2025. 
3.8.2 The Committee was also informed that the Cens us 2027 is to be conducted in two phases, 
viz ., (a) House listing & Housing Census during April- September, 2026 and (b) Population 
Enumeration during February, 2027 followed by a rev isional round during first week of March, 
2027. The Ministry further stated that both phases of Census 2027 will be conducted during FY 
2026–27, necessitating a substantial allocation dur ing the year. The Union Cabinet has approved 
a total budget of ₹11,718.24 crore for the exercise , and approximately 30 lakh Census 
functionaries will be deployed across the country. Funds will be allocated to States/UTs towards 
honorarium, training and technical assistance, ther eby justifying the enhanced provision in BE 
2026–27. 
3.8.3 The Ministry informed that Census 2027 will b e India’s first digital Census. Data will be 
collected through secure mobile applications suppor ted by digital maps and real-time monitoring 

29 
 
systems. Enumerators will conduct door-to-door canv assing using mobile applications available 
in Hindi, English and 14 other languages to ensure complete coverage. The public will also have 
the option of self-enumeration through an online po rtal. The calibrated adoption of digital 
technology is aimed at improving operational effici ency, enhancing data quality and enabling 
faster dissemination of Census results.

3.8.4 The Ministry informed that strong data securi ty measures are being implemented for 
Census 2027. The Ministry stated that the adoption of digital tools, encrypted transmission and 
real-time supervision are expected to enhance data integrity, accuracy and timely dissemination 
of Census data. The measures and tools include: 
 End-to-end encryption of data collected through mobile applications and self-
enumeration portals; 
 Secure authentication through verified digital IDs for enumerators; 
 Real-time monitoring by dedicated cyber security teams; 

30 
 
 Public awareness campaigns to prevent misuse and phishing; and 
 Strengthening of servers, firewalls and backup systems to handle large-scale digital data 
securely. 
Observations/Recommendations 
3.8.5 The Committee notes the substantial increase in allocation to ₹6,000 crore in BE 
2026–27 on account of full-fledged Census 2027 oper ations and appreciates the 
Government’s preparedness to undertake India’s firs t fully digital Census. The Committee 
is of the view that given the scale and sensitivity  of the exercise, meticulous planning, inter-
governmental coordination and technological robustn ess are critical for its successful 
conduct. The Committee recommends that the Ministry  may ensure timely finalisation of 
all preparatory activities, including training of a pproximately 30 lakh Census 
functionaries, deployment of secure digital infrast ructure and seamless coordination with 
State Governments to avoid operational bottlenecks.  The Committee further recommends 
that a clear dissemination calendar may be prepared  for release of provisional and final 
Census data so as to facilitate evidence-based policy formulation at the earliest. 
3.8.6 The Committee also emphasises the importance of robust data protection 
safeguards, including end-to-end encryption, cyber- security audits and real-time 
monitoring mechanisms, to preserve data integrity and public trust. In view of the adoption 
of digital tools and self-enumeration facilities, t he Committee recommends that adequate 
public awareness campaigns be undertaken to ensure informed participation and prevent 
misuse of digital platforms. 
3.9  HOME GUARDS  
3.9.1 The Ministry informed the Committee that the increase between BE 2025–26 and RE 
2025–26, as well as BE 2026–27, is due to additiona l demand by the Directorate General, Fire 
Services, Civil Defence and Home Guards (DGCDFS&HG)  for reimbursement to States/UTs 
and clearing of backlog claims. The Committee was a pprised that Home Guards constitute a 
voluntary force raised by States and Union Territor y Administrations under a broad policy 
framework laid down by the Ministry of Home Affairs . The force supplements State/UT police 
forces in maintaining law and order, traffic manage ment, protection of public property, guarding 
borders and performing election-related duties. The  central provision is meant for assisting 
States/UTs in raising, training and equipping Home Guards. 
3.9.2 In response to the Committee’s query regardin g enhancement of working conditions, 
training, equipment and deployment constraints, the  Ministry informed that the following 
measures have been proposed/initiated by Directorat e General (Fire Services, Civil Defence and 
Home Guards), MHA. As Home Guards fall under the ju risdiction of respective States/UTs, 
implementation would be undertaken by them: 

31 
 
(i) Introduction of Digital Platform - “Seva Path” 
The proposed Model Home Guards Bill, 2025 envisages  a dedicated digital platform for 
transparent and efficient allotment of duties. The platform provides for term-wise 
engagement of Home Guards for a five-year period, e nsuring equitable deployment, 
improved workforce planning and enhanced accountability. 
(ii) Digitalisation of Wage Disbursement 
States/UTs may introduce digital systems for disbur sement of wages/honorarium through 
Direct Benefit Transfer (DBT) to ensure transparenc y and timely payments. Coordination 
with Government banks is also proposed to extend ac count-linked benefits on lines similar 
to those available to Armed Forces/CAPFs. 
(iii) 
Group Medical Insurance and Health Coverage 
States/UTs may introduce Group Medical Insurance sc hemes or extend coverage under 
existing Central/State health schemes such as Ayush man Bharat to ensure comprehensive 
health protection for Home Guards. 
(iv) 
Modernisation of Training Modules 
A review of training syllabi is under process to al ign with contemporary requirements, with 
emphasis on disaster management, community engageme nt, internal security support and 
awareness of flagship schemes. A conference with St ate/UT authorities has already been 
convened in this regard. 
(v) 
Integration with SDRF as First Responders 
It has been recommended that Home Guards may be tra ined and utilised as first responders 
at the district level, particularly in disaster pre paredness and response, considering their 
wide presence across districts. 
(vi) 
Review of Uniform and Equipment Policy 
A national-level committee has been constituted to standardise uniforms and modernise 
equipment, ensuring climate suitability and functio nal adequacy for duties relating to 
disasters, elections and internal security. 
 
Observations/Recommendations 
3.9.3  The Committee notes that although the alloca tion under the head “Home Guards” 
has improved in BE 2026–27, it remains modest in co mparison to the expanding role of 
Home Guards in elections, disaster response and int ernal security duties. The Committee 
recommends that the Ministry, in consultation with States/UTs, may undertake a 
comprehensive assessment of financial and operation al requirements so that central 
assistance is commensurate with the increasing depl oyment responsibilities of the force. 
The Committee further recommends that proposed digi tal initiatives, including the “Seva 
Path” platform and digitalisation of wage disbursem ent, may be implemented in a time-

32 
 
bound manner across States/UTs to ensure transparen cy, equitable deployment and timely 
disbursement of honorarium through Direct Benefit Transfer (DBT). 
3.9.4  The Committee further recommends that modern isation of training modules and 
integration of Home Guards as first responders with  State Disaster Response Forces 
(SDRFs) may be institutionalised through structured  joint exercises and periodic refresher 
training. Emphasis may also be placed on provision of climate-suitable uniforms, modern 
protective gear and essential equipment to enable e ffective discharge of duties during 
disasters, elections and internal security operatio ns, thereby enhancing operational 
efficiency and morale of the force.

CHAPTER -IV 
 
DEMAND NO. 51 - POLICE 
 
4.1 OVERVIEW 
 
4.1.1 The Demand No.51 - Police, contains budgetary  provisions for the Central Armed Police 
Forces (CAPFs), Assam Rifles and National Security Guards. Departmental Accounting 
Organization of CAPFs, National Intelligence Grid ( NATGRID), Delhi Police, Intelligence 
Bureau, Bureau of Immigration, Narcotics Control Bu reau, National Investigation Agency and 
other Central Police Organizations, as well as for certain schemes administered by Ministry of 
Home Affairs, such as Infrastructure projects of Ce ntral Armed Police forces (CAPFs) and Delhi 
Police, Development of Border Infrastructure and Ma nagement, Mission Mode Project on 
Immigration, Visa and Foreigners Registration & Tra cking (IVFRT), Schemes for Safety of 
Women financed from Nirbhaya Fund, Grants to States  for raising and maintaining India Reserve 
Battalions and the Centrally Sponsored Schemes viz . Umbrella Scheme of “Modernization of 
Police Forces” and “Vibrant village Programme”. Pro vision under this Grant also caters to the 
needs for Welfare Grants of CAPFs, Research etc.  
4.1.2 As far as Police Grant is concerned, the allo cations under the ‘Revenue Section’ are 
predominantly for Salaries, allowances, Travel Expenses and other administrative expenses of the 
CAPFs, CPOs, Delhi Police and other organizations a nd Grants-in-aid to States/UT Governments 
under various Schemes like ‘Modernization of Police  Forces, vibrant village Programme, 
Schemes financed from Nirbhaya Fund for Safety of Women, etc.  
4.1.3 Under the ‘Capital Section’, the budgetary pr ovisions for construction of Residential and 
Office Buildings by the Central Armed Police Forces  and Central Police Organizations, 
procurement of motor vehicles, machinery & equipmen t, creation of infrastructure for securing 
India’s International Borders, 
viz . Border Outposts, Border Roads, Flood Lighting, Fe ncing, etc.

4.2 CENTRAL ARMED POLICE FORCES (CAPFs) 
4.2.1 The provision under this head relates to meet ing the establishment expenditure of the 
Central Armed Police Forces (CAPFs), namely, the Ce ntral Reserve Police Force (CRPF), 
National Security Guard (NSG), Border Security Forc e (BSF), Indo-Tibetan Border Police 
(ITBP), Central Industrial Security Force (CISF), Assam Rifles and Sashastra Seema Bal (SSB). 
4.2.2 Assam Rifles, Border Security Force, Indo-Tib etan Border Police and Sashastra Seema 
Bal are known as Border Guarding Forces and have be en entrusted with the responsibility of 
securing India’s international borders with Myanmar , Bangladesh, Pakistan, China, Nepal and 
Tibet. National Security Guards is an elite securit y agency specializing in Counter Terrorism 
operations. Central Reserve Police Force is the pre mier police force deployed in various States 
and supplements to the State police forces for general law & order duties, and for maintenance of 
law and order during elections, etc.; its specializ ed battalions of Rapid Action Force (RAF) are 
mandated for maintaining communal harmony in the co untry; its commando force, Commando 
Battalion for Resolute Action (COBRA), are deployed  for Counter Insurgency and Anti-Naxal 
operations in LWE affected areas, J&K and other places. The Central Industrial Security Force is 
deployed on security duties at most of the Airports  in the country, offices / factories of Public 
Sector Undertakings, Central / State Government age ncies and other installations as decided by 
the Government of India based on security threat as sessment. The budgetary provision for 
CAPFs for the year 2025-26 is given in the following table:- 
Allocation made to CAPFs for the past 3 years

these CAPFs. Both Revenue and Capital provisions include outlays for essential items specific to 
Armed Forces, i.e., Arms & Ammunition, Clothing & T entage and IT equipment under Revenue 

35 
 
Section and provisions for Motor Vehicles and Machi neries & Equipment under the Capital 
Section (collectively referred to as ‘Provisioning Items’).  
4.2.4 The Ministry has explained that the variation  between RE 2025–26 and BE 2025–26 
under the Central Armed Police Forces (CAPFs) head is primarily on account of requirement of 
additional funds under allowances, material and sup plies, travel expenses, machinery & 
equipment and Information & Communication Technolog y (ICT). With regard to the variation 
between BE 2026–27 and BE 2025–26, the Ministry has  stated that the enhanced provision is 
attributable to anticipated expenditure under allow ances, medical expenditure, minor works, 
provisioning and other establishment-related expens es, along with increased allocation towards 
ICT and machinery & equipment. The additional alloc ation has been made keeping in view the 
operational and administrative requirements of the Forces. 
Observation/ Recommendation 
4.2.5 The Committee notes that the allocation for C entral Armed Police Forces (CAPFs) 
has shown a steady increase over the last three yea rs, with the total outlay rising from 
₹1,04,824.10 crores in Actuals 2024–25 to ₹1,16,789 .30 crores in BE 2026–27. The 
Committee observes that the increase is largely att ributable to higher provisions under 
allowances, medical expenditure, provisioning items , ICT and Machinery and Equipment, 
reflecting the growing operational commitments and administrative requirements of the 
Forces. The Committee further notes that while Reve nue expenditure constitutes the 
predominant share of the allocation, enhanced provi sions have also been made under the 
Capital Section towards machinery & equipment and motor vehicles.  
In view of the expanding role of CAPFs in border ma nagement, counter-terrorism, 
internal security and election duties, the Committe e recommends that sustained emphasis 
may be placed on modernisation of equipment, techno logy upgradation and infrastructure 
strengthening to enhance operational efficiency and  response capability. The Ministry may 
periodically review the ratio of Revenue to Capital  expenditure with a view to ensure the 
capital investments in modern equipment, mobility a nd surveillance systems keep pace 
with evolving security challenges.  
4.3 REPRESENTATION OF WOMEN IN CAPFS 
4.3.1 The Committee sought to know about the repres entation of women in all Central Armed 
Police Forces (CAPFs). The Ministry of Home Affairs  (MHA) has stated that, in January 2016, a 
decision was taken to reserve 33% of posts at the C onstable level for women in the Central 
Reserve Police Force (CRPF) and the Central Industr ial Security Force (CISF), and 14–15% of 
posts at the Constable level in the border guarding  forces, namely the Border Security Force 
(BSF), Sashastra Seema Bal (SSB), and Indo-Tibetan Border Police (ITBP). 

36 
 
4.3.2 The Ministry further stated that the Recruitm ent Rules in the Forces are gender-neutral 
and recruitment is carried out through the Union Pu blic Service Commission (UPSC), Staff 
Selection Commission (SSC), nodal force-based commo n recruitment, and departmental in-
house recruitment. Timely recruitment is undertaken  to fill vacant posts reserved for women. 
Female candidates are exempted from payment of appl ication fees during recruitment and one 
female member is detailed as member of the board fo r making recruitment of women personnel. 
Women personnel are provided equal opportunities in  career progression, including promotion 
and seniority, in accordance with the Recruitment Rules, on par with male counterparts. 
4.3.3 The Ministry has also informed that, as on 31 .12.2025, the total strength of women 
personnel in CAPFs and Assam Rifles was 46,992 agai nst a sanctioned strength of 10,73,563, 
constituting 4.37% of the total strength. During th e last five years, the strength of women 
personnel increased from 34,222 in 2021 to 35,074 i n 2022, 41,606 in 2023, 42,470 in 2024 and 
46,992 in 2025. Force-wise, the number of women per sonnel in 2025 was 10,277 in CRPF, 
12,776 in BSF, 12,665 in CISF, 4,522 in ITBP, 4,240  in SSB, and 2,512 in Assam Rifles. 
However, the strength of Women in CAPFs and Assam R ifles during the last five-year is as 
under :-

4.3.4 With regard to leadership roles, the Ministry  has stated that women are being appointed 
to higher positions, including Assistant Commandant s in CAPFs and Assam Rifles, at par with 
their male counterparts and are being provided equal opportunities to assume leadership roles. 
Observation/ Recommendation 
4.3.5 The Committee notes the steps taken by the Go vernment to enhance the 
representation of women in CAPFs, including reserva tion of posts and other supportive 
measures. However, the Committee observes that, in addition to recruitment, sustained 
improvement in women’s representation also depends on reducing attrition. The 
Committee also emphasises the importance of increas ing women’s participation in the 
officer cadre so as to ensure their presence at dec ision-making and leadership levels. The 

37 
 
Committee believes that improving service condition s, enhancing retention, and promoting 
greater induction of women into the officer cadre w ill contribute significantly to achieve 
higher and sustained representation of women in CAPFs. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Gover nment may take focused 
measures to create a more women-friendly working en vironment in the Forces so as to 
improve retention of women personnel. The Ministry may strengthen institutional support 
systems, ensure availability of adequate facilities , and address workplace concerns 
effectively so that women personnel are able to continue in service without undue hardship. 
The Ministry may explore flexible deployment option s or soft postings during specific life 
stages to enhance retention.  
4.4 CADRE REVIEW OF CAPFS 
4.4.1  The Committee sought details of the outcomes of the  recent cadre review for CAPFs, 
including steps taken to address promotion stagnati on and strengthen organizational capacity 
along with timelines. The Ministry has informed tha t, in Assam Rifles, the Cadre Review of 
Group ‘B’ and ‘C’ posts has been approved on 14 th  January 2026. The review provides for 
rationalized rank structure for all trades and categories, improved career progression and removal 
of stagnation in promotions across various trades. It also includes abolition or merger of 
redundant trades based on functional requirements t o improve efficiency. In cadres where 
promotional vacancies of Centrally Controlled Categ ory personnel have increased and their 
Recruitment Rules are available, promotions will be  issued by conducting supplementary 
Departmental Promotion Committees (DPCs) (wherever necessary) after formulation of the new 
Peace Establishment. However, where Recruitment Rul es are not yet available, promotions will 
be issued after their approval and formulation of t he new Peace Establishment. In the General 
Duty Cadre in which promotional vacancies have been  increased, promotion will be issued by 
conducting supplementary DPC post approval of new P eace Establishment.and in the Unit 
Tradesmen Cadre in which vacancies have been increa sed, newly introduced, promotion will be 
issued by conducting DPC post approval of fresh Recruitment Rules and Peace Establishment. 
4.4.2  The Ministry has further informed that, in Border S ecurity Force (BSF), the Government 
has approved cadre reviews of six cadres, namely Gr oup ‘B’ and ‘C’ General Duty Cadre, Water 
Wing Cadre, Ministerial Cadre, Stenographic Cadre, Para-Medical Cadre and Veterinary Cadre, 
resulting in the creation of 4,650 additional posts . These cadre reviews have addressed issues of 
career progression and stagnation while enhancing o perational efficiency and functional 
requirements of the Force. As a result, 19,816 pers onnel have been promoted to higher ranks. 
The creation of new posts has also provided employment opportunities.  
4.4.3  In Central Industrial Security Force (CISF), the Mi nistry has stated that cadre reviews are 
conducted periodically to address promotion stagnat ion and strengthen organizational capacity. 
The last Cadre Review was conducted in 2018, during which 25 Group ‘A’ posts were created. A 

38 
 
Cadre Review Committee has been constituted to form ulate a comprehensive Cadre Review to 
address stagnation and organizational capacity.  
4.4.4 
 In Central Reserve Police Force (CRPF), the Ministr y has informed that periodic Cadre 
Reviews are conducted to create promotional avenues , address stagnation and ensure structured 
career progression. In 2025, the second Cadre Revie w of the Stenographer Cadre was approved 
on 28 August 2025, under which 18 posts of Inspecto r/Stenographer were created or upgraded, 
improving promotional prospects and availability of supervisory manpower. The following cadre 
reviews are under process: fourth Cadre Review of G roup ‘A’ General Duty Officers; second 
Cadre Reviews of Group ‘A’, ‘B’ and ‘C’ Para-Medica l Cadre and Ministerial Cadre. Second 
cadre review of Signal Cadre and Armourer Cadre; an d a limited Cadre Review of Group ‘C’ 
Motor Transport Cadre.  
4.4.5 
 The Ministry has informed that in Indo-Tibetan Bord er Police (ITBP) the last Cadre 
Review was conducted during 2019 and 2020, during which 130 Group ‘A’ posts were created to 
address promotion and stagnation and strengthen organizational capacity. 
4.4.6  In Sashastra Seema Bal (SSB), the Ministry has info rmed that the Government has 
approved cadre reviews of four cadres, namely Medic al Officers Cadre, Para-Medical Cadre, 
Tradesmen and Engineering Cadre and Stenographer Ca dre, resulting in the creation of 89 
additional posts. These reviews have addressed care er progression and stagnation while 
enhancing operational efficiency and functional req uirements of the Force. The creation of 
additional posts has also provided employment opportunities.  
Observation/ Recommendation 
4.4.7 The Committee notes the Cadre Review measures  undertaken across various 
CAPFs and Assam Rifles, including creation of addit ional posts, rationalization of rank 
structures and steps to address stagnation in promo tion. The Committee observes that 
while these measures have facilitated career progre ssion in certain cadres, several reviews 
are still under process and their benefits will mat erialize only after timely implementation, 
including approval of Recruitment Rules, formulatio n of new Peace Establishments and 
conduct of Departmental Promotion Committees meetin gs. The Committee has also 
observed that there are concerns regarding stagnati on in promotion from the rank of 
Inspector (Group ‘B’) to Assistant Commandant (Grou p ‘A’) in the Central Industrial 
Security Force (CISF).  
The Committee recommends that prolonged stagnation at supervisory levels may 
adversely impact morale, motivation and career prog ression of personnel. The Ministry 
may undertake a comprehensive cadre review of the c oncerned ranks to examine 
promotional avenues, vacancy position and promotion  timelines. Appropriate measures, 
including rationalisation of promotional quotas, ti mely conduct of Departmental 
Promotion Committees meetings and review of cadre s tructure, may be considered to 

39 
 
ensure a fair and time-bound career progression fra mework. Steps may also be taken to 
ensure parity in career progression among officials /officers of different CAPFs. The 
Limited Departmental Competitive Examination (LDCE)  for promotion to the rank of 
Assistant Commandant may also be conducted through the Union Public Service 
Commission (UPSC) in CAPFs, on the lines of the exi sting arrangement in the Central 
Industrial Security Force (CISF), so as to ensure g reater transparency, uniformity and 
credibility in the selection process. 
4.5 WELFARE MEASURES FOR CAPF 
4.5.1 The Committee noted that allocation under “We lfare Grant and Miscellaneous” has 
declined from ₹1,422.65 crores in RE 2025–26 to ₹1, 090.00 crores in BE 2026–27. The Ministry 
informed that the primary reason for the reduction is that the provision for Medical Treatment 
(Ayushman CAPF) in BE 2026–27 has been retained at ₹700 crores, in line with BE 2025–26. 
The Ministry stated that expenditure under medical treatment is demand-driven and cannot be 
accurately predicted in advance. During 2025–26, th e allocation was supplemented at the RE 
stage based on expenditure trends. Any additional r equirement in 2026–27 will similarly be 
projected at RE stage or through Supplementary Grants. 
4.5.2 The Committee was informed that CAPFs have au gmented their health infrastructure 
through recruitment of doctors and paramedical staf f, construction of new 10, 20 and 50-bedded 
hospitals and upgradation of various field hospital s. Preventive and fitness-based medical 
initiatives, including sports, yoga protocol, physi cal training and mental health interventions, 
have been given emphasis. Further, the Central Arme d Police Forces Institute of Medical 
Sciences (CAPFIMS) has been proposed as a tertiary and speciality healthcare facility for CAPF 
personnel and their dependents. Under the Ayushman CAPF initiative, cashless and paperless 
treatment is available in 2,027 CGHS-empanelled hos pitals and 33,135 AB-PMJAY empanelled 
hospitals that have signed MoUs with the National H ealth Authority. Approximately 42 lakh 
Ayushman CAPF IDs have been generated. Retired pers onnel and their spouses are eligible for 
CGHS/CPMF hospital facilities or a medical allowance of ₹1,000 per month. 
4.5.3 The Ministry further apprised the Committee t hat comprehensive welfare measures are in 
place for families of CAPF and Assam Rifles personn el who lost their lives in the line of duty. 
The Welfare and Rehabilitation Board (WARB), establ ished in 2007, oversees welfare and 
rehabilitation of retired CAPF personnel, their fam ilies and dependents of deceased or disabled 
personnel. WARB functions through 06 Central Welfar e Officers, 32 State Welfare Officers and 
201 District Welfare Officers across the country. N ext of Kins (NoKs) of CAPF and Assam 
Rifles personnel killed in action are provided Cent ral ex-gratia compensation (₹35 lakh for death 
on active duty and ₹25 lakh for death on duty), fin ancial assistance of ₹25 lakh from the “Bharat 
Ke Veer” portal, insurance coverage under CAPF sala ry package schemes, compassionate 
appointment, allotment of retail petroleum outlets and air/rail fare concessions. It is ensured that 
NoKs receive a minimum of ₹1 crore from various sou rces. As on 23.01.2026, ₹545.22 crores 
has been contributed through Bharat Ke Veer and ass istance has been provided to 635 NoKs. 

40 
 
Liberalized/Extraordinary Family Pension under CCS (EOP) Rules, 2023 is admissible. 
Additional assistance includes scholarship under Pr ime Minister's Scholarship Scheme(PMSS) 
(₹3,000 per month for girls and ₹2,500 per month fo r boys), reservation of 26 MBBS and 3 BDS 
seats for CAPF wards and support under Contributory Welfare Fund. 
4.5.4 For rehabilitation and re-employment, WARB co nsults with private companies and Public 
Sector Undertakings and circulates vacancy notifica tions to retired personnel. The “CAPF 
Punarvaas” scheme links the WARB website with the P rivate Security Agencies (Regulation) 
Act (PSARA) website to facilitate re-employment of retired CAPF/AR personnel in private 
security agencies. Counselling and employment suppo rt are extended, particularly to widows of 
personnel killed in action. 
4.5.5 Other welfare initiatives include enhancement  of retirement age from 57 to 60 years for 
ranks below DIG, grant of Risk and Hardship Allowan ce for deployment in J&K, Ladakh and 
LWE areas, additional HRA for personnel posted in K ashmir Valley, air courier services in 
difficult areas, air travel facilities for non-enti tled personnel and the CAPF e-Awas Portal for 
online allotment of residential accommodation. The Ministry informed in its presentation that as 
on 31.12.2025, the total authorised dwelling units (DUs) for CAPFs, Assam Rifles (AR) and 
NSG stand at 2,68,346, against which 1,35,544 dwell ing units are presently available. In 
addition, 10,430 dwelling units are under construct ion. The housing satisfaction level has been 
indicated at 50.14% in respect of available accommo dation and 54.03% considering ongoing 
construction. The Kendriya Police Kalyan Bhandar (K PKB) provides concessional canteen 
facilities with 50% GST concession w.e.f. 01.04.202 4. Stress management initiatives including 
“Art of Living” programmes and structured grievance redressal mechanisms are also in place. 
Observation / Recommendation  
4.5.6 The Committee appreciates the comprehensive w elfare framework put in place for 
families of CAPF and Assam Rifles personnel killed in action and for rehabilitation of 
retired personnel. The Committee notes the institut ional mechanism established through 
the Welfare and Rehabilitation Board (WARB) and the  deployment of Central, State and 
District Welfare Officers across the country. The C ommittee recommends that the 
Ministry may evolve a structured outcome-monitoring mechanism to periodically assess the 
effectiveness of financial assistance, pensionary b enefits, scholarship schemes and re-
employment initiatives, so as to ensure timely deli very and long-term socio-economic 
stability of beneficiary families. 
4.5.7 The Committee recommends that re-employment i nitiatives under the “CAPF 
Punarvaas” scheme may be strengthened through great er coordination with Public Sector 
Undertakings and private sector entities, and that data regarding placements and self-
employment outcomes may be periodically compiled an d reviewed. Special emphasis may 
be placed on skill development and counselling supp ort for widows and dependents to 
enable sustainable livelihood opportunities. 

41 
 
4.5.8 The Committee observes that the availability of housing units remains significantly 
below the authorised strength, with only 1,35,544 dwelling units presently in place against a 
sanctioned capacity of 2,68,346 units. In addition,  10,430 units are currently under 
construction, which, when completed, will marginally improve the situation. At present, the 
housing satisfaction level stands at 50.14%, reflec ting the considerable shortfall in meeting 
the sanctioned requirements. Upon completion of the  ongoing projects, this figure is 
projected to rise to 54.03%, indicating some progre ss but still underscoring the persistent 
gap between authorised strength and actual availabi lity. The existing housing gap requires 
sustained and time-bound augmentation.  
The Committee recommends that personnel deployed in  hard-duty, border and 
high-risk operational areas may be accorded priorit y in allotment of residential 
accommodation. Completion of ongoing housing projec ts may be closely monitored and 
that a phased roadmap may be prepared to progressiv ely enhance housing satisfaction 
levels across the Forces. 
4.5.9 Further, the Committee recommends that given the increasing operational stress 
and deployment in difficult terrains, access to qua lity and timely medical care assumes 
critical importance for CAPF personnel and their fa milies. Periodic review of empanelled 
hospitals, claim settlement timelines and beneficiary coverage may be undertaken to ensure 
comprehensive and timely medical support to serving  and retired personnel and their 
dependents. 
4.5.10 The Committee also take notes of the enhance ment of Central ex-gratia 
compensation and the assurance of a minimum financi al support package from various 
sources. While acknowledging the existing structure , the Committee recommends that the 
Ministry may examine the feasibility of evolving a broad and simplified categorisation of 
ex-gratia compensation so as to ensure clarity, uni formity and equity. The framework may 
remain sensitive to operational realities while avoiding over-fragmentation of categories.

4.6.2 The Ministry informed that filling up of vaca ncies is a continuous process and several 
measures have been undertaken to expedite recruitme nt. Directions have been issued to 
undertake recruitment in Non-General Duty cadres in  a time-bound manner. Yearly recruitment 
for the post of Constable (GD) is being conducted through the Staff Selection Commission (SSC) 
under a Memorandum of Understanding. A Nodal Force has been designated for coordinated 
recruitment in the ranks of Constable (GD), Sub-Ins pector (GD) and Assistant Commandant 
(GD). Radio Frequency Identification (RFID) technol ogy is being used during the conduct of 
Physical Standard Test (PST) and Physical Efficienc y Test (PET) to enhance transparency and 
efficiency. Time taken in Detailed Medical Examinat ion (DME) and Review Medical 
Examination (RME) has been reduced to expedite the recruitment. Cut-off marks for shortlisting 
of candidates for Constable (GD) have been rational ised to address category-wise shortfalls. 
Regular meetings of Departmental Promotion Committe es (DPCs) are being conducted to fill 
promotional vacancies. 
4.6.3 The Committee sought details regarding propos als for raising additional battalions in 
CAPFs during the last three years. The Ministry inf ormed that the following proposals were 
received: 
 CISF: 03 Battalions - Sanctioned and raised  
 ITBP: 07 Battalions and 01 Sector Headquarters - Sanctioned and raised  
 SSB: 01 Sector and 12 Battalions - Under consideration 
 BSF: 02 additional Sector Headquarters and 16 Battalions under consideration 
 CRPF: 20 additional Battalions under consideration 
 Assam Rifles: 05 Battalions under consideration 
The total expenditure incurred so far amounts to ₹3,086.83 crore. 
4.6.4 The Ministry informed that several measures h ave been taken to reduce deployment-
related stress among CAPF and AR personnel. Annual Medical Examination (AME), including 
psychological assessment, is conducted. Personnel w ith mental health concerns are provided 
treatment and counselling and referred to specialis ts where necessary. Mental health services are 
also covered under Ayushman CAPF.Regular interactio n of officers with troops, regulation of 
duty hours to ensure adequate rest, improved medica l facilities and organisation of yoga, 
meditation and “Art of Living” programmes are under taken to enhance well-being. The Art of 
Living Foundation conducts stress management course s annually for 200 companies in CAPFs 
and ARs every year. The aim of program is to reduce  stress among personnel deployed in J&K, 

43 
 
North-East and LWE areas. CAPFs and AR have signed MoUs with variousmental health 
institutions and maintain dedicated psychiatric facilities in Composite Hospitals. 
Observation / Recommendation 
4.6.5 
 The Committee notes with concern that the total vac ancies across CAPFs and 
Assam Rifles stand at 93,139, with significant gaps  particularly in the Other Ranks (ORs) 
category. While appreciating the measures undertake n to expedite recruitment through 
coordination with SSC/UPSC, time-bound medical exam inations and designation of nodal-
force for coordinating recruitment, the Committee i s of the view that sustained high 
vacancy levels may adversely impact operational eff ectiveness, increase deployment 
pressure and affect morale of personnel. The Commit tee recommends that the Ministry 
may prepare a time-bound plan to fill vacancy, with  clearly defined annual targets and 
periodic monitoring at the highest level.  
4.6.6 The Committee appreciates the initiatives tak en towards stress management, 
including Annual Medical Examination with psycholog ical assessment, Ayushman CAPF 
coverage for mental health, MoUs with specialised i nstitutions and conduct of yoga and 
mental well-being programmes.  
However, considering the high operational stress an d deployment in difficult 
terrains, the Committee recommends that mental heal th concerns, particularly among 
Other Ranks deployed in field formations, require s ustained and institutional attention. 
Structured mental health screening, confidential co unselling mechanisms and strengthened 
field-level psychiatric support may be provided acr oss formations. Data relating to stress-
related cases, counselling interventions and rehabi litation outcomes may be periodically 
reviewed to assess the effectiveness of existing me asures and to ensure long-term 
psychological resilience of the Forces. 
4.7 MODERNIZATION PLAN IV FOR CAPFS 
4.7.1  The Modernization Plan-IV for Central Armed Police Forces (CAPFs) aims to upgrade 
weapons, equipment, infrastructure and technology t o enhance operational effectiveness, 
mobility and preparedness. Its purpose is to streng then internal security, border management and 
counter-terror operations in line with evolving sec urity challenges. The Committee noted that 
allocation under Modernization Plan-IV for CAPFs ha s declined from ₹610.79 crores in RE 
2025-26 to ₹343.66 crores in BE 2026-27. The Minist ry informed that the Modernization Plan-
IV was approved by the Cabinet Committee on Securit y in February 2022 with a total outlay of 
₹1,523 crores for implementation from 01.02.2022 to  31.03.2026. Due to issuance of Scheme 
Guidelines in May 2022, expenditure during 2021-22 was Nil anda limited expenditureduring 
2022-23. 

44 
 
4.7.2 The Ministry further stated that most of the items under Mod Plan-IV are highly technical 
in nature. They are not available in domestic marke ts off the shelf. Further, procurement 
procedures for the same are rather cumbersome and t ime-consuming. Hence, delivery period of 
these items are longer resulting in long gestation period and delayed procurement process. Also, 
due to incomplete documents/non-fulfilment of tende r requirements, tenders were being 
scrapped. Since, the implementation period of Moder nization Plan IV ends on 31.03.2026, 
hence, CAPFs have expedited their procurement proce ss in the current financial year 2025-26 
and most of the Tenders/Bids are either already fin alized or likely to be finalized by the end of 
the current financial year 2025- 26. Accordingly, C APFs have been allocated ₹610.79 crores 
under RE. 2025-26 i.e. an increase of ₹257.87 crore s over and above BE 2025-26 (₹ 352.92 
crores). Further, the funds proposed for BE 2026-27  under the Scheme caters to only committed 
liabilities which will be booked in 2026-27 owing t o their delivery falling in FY 2026-27, hence, 
amount of ₹343.66 crores are allocated under BE 2026-27. 
4.7.3 The Ministry further informed that to ensure modernization, CAPFs have also been 
allocated funds under General budget heads to upgra de their inventory and make good of their 
deficiency of weapons, surveillance system, Drone T echnology, Upgraded Communication 
System, Bullet Proof vehicles, Mine Protected Vehicles, ECC&E Clothing items etc. 
4.7.4 The Scheme aims to equip CAPFs (Assam Rifles,  BSF, CISF, CRPF, ITBP, NSG and 
SSB) with modern weapons and equipment suited to di verse deployment theatres. Approved 
items include: 
 Weapons: Grenade Launchers, Rocket Launchers, Sniper Rifles; 
 Surveillance & Reconnaissance: Night Vision Devices, Unmanned Aerial Vehicles 
(UAVs), Ground Penetrating Radar, Anti-Drone Equipment; 
 Specialized Vehicles: Mine Protected Vehicles, Bullet Proof Vehicles; 
 Communication equipments: Digital Radio Sets, Network-in-Box, Satellite Phones; 
 Medical Equipment: CT Scanners, MRI Scanners; 
 Training Infrastructure: Composite Indoor Shooting Ranges; and 
 Upgraded IT Solutions. 
4.7.5 The Ministry stated that the plan seeks to br idge the technological gap between existing 
inventory and contemporary operational requirements . The Lead Force concept for procurement 
of common equipment continues to be followed to ens ure efficiency. Further,the details of fund 
utilized during 2024-25 and 2025-26 (till 31.12.2025) is as under: 
Fund utilized during 2024-25 and 2025-26 under Modernization Plan IV for CAPFs

4.7.6 The Committee, further, sought to know about the measures taken to upgrade medical 
infrastructure and evacuation protocols for CAPF pe rsonnel deployed in high-stress/high altitude 
sectors to which ministry informed that CAPFs, AR a nd NSG have undertaken multiple 
measures to strengthen medical infrastructure and e vacuation systems for personnel deployed in 
high-stress and high-altitude areas. Institutional mechanisms include Annual Medical 
Examination (AME) with SHAPE assessment, psychologi cal counselling, grievance redressal 
systems, buddy-pair monitoring, stress-management w orkshops and appointment of 
psychologists on honorarium basis. MoUs have been s igned with the National Institute of Yoga, 
and specific yoga protocols have been made mandator y before deployment. As on date, 3,789 
Master Trainers and 29,384 Yoga Trainers have been trained, including 253 women Master 
Trainers. Medical evacuation facilities include hel icopter/air ambulance evacuation, air 
evacuation from Border Out Posts (BOPs) in coordina tion with the Indian Air Force, road 
evacuation with medical teams and provision of Adva nced Life Support ambulances. Hyperbaric 
Oxygen Therapy (HBOT) facilities are also available in high-altitude sectors.

Observations/ Recommendations 
 
4.7.8 The Committee notes that Unmanned Aerial Vehi cles (UAVs) and Anti-Drone 
equipment form an integral component of Modernizati on Plan-IV, particularly in view of 
emerging threats along international borders and in  internal security concerns. 
Considering the increasing use of drones for survei llance, smuggling and hostile activities, 
the Committee recommends that procurement and deployment of drone and counter-drone 
systems may be accorded priority and fast-tracked, especially in border guarding forces. 
The indigenous technology solutions may be encourag ed, and integrated command-and-
control mechanisms may be developed to ensure real- time surveillance, intelligence sharing 
and operational response across formations. Further , special emphasis should be laid in 
training capacity building measures related to oper ation and handling of UAVs and 
counter-drone systems. 
 
4.7.9 The Committee observes that Modernization Pla n-IV aims to bridge the 
technological gap between existing inventory and co ntemporary operational requirements. 
However, procurement delays due to technical comple xities and tender-related issues have 
resulted in delays in implementation.  
 
 The Committee recommends that a streamlined procur ement framework with 
better technical vetting, advance planning and moni toring of delivery schedules may be 
initiated to avoid last-minute rush and accumulatio n of committed liabilities. Sustained 

47 
 
capital allocation under both scheme and general bu dget heads may be ensured to 
progressively modernise weapons, communication syst ems, surveillance equipment and 
protective gear so as to enhance operational prepar edness of CAPFs in diverse and 
challenging environments.
