Observation/ Recommendation 
4.8.6 The Committee notes that increasing represent ation of women in police forces is 
essential for promoting gender-sensitive policing, improving public trust and enhancing 
response to crimes against women and vulnerable sec tions. The Committee is of the view 
that Delhi, being the National Capital with a diver se and dense population, requires a 
strengthened and balanced force structure reflectin g adequate representation of women 
personnel at all levels.  
The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Minis try, in coordination with 
Delhi Police, may prepare a phased and time-bound r oadmap to enhance the strength of 
women personnel to at least 33 percent of the total  sanctioned strength. Focused 
recruitment drives, adequate training capacity, pro vision of suitable infrastructure 
including separate accommodation and facilities, an d enabling service conditions may be 
ensured to achieve this objective in a sustainable manner. 
4.8.7 The Committee observes that hesitation among citizens to visit police stations often 
stems from perceptions of inaccessibility, lack of transparency and discomfort in 
interacting with personnel. Such barriers have pote ntial to discourage reporting of crime, 
weaken community participation and reduce trust in law enforcement.  
The Committee, therefore, recommends that Delhi Pol ice may adopt a citizen-
centric policing model to make police stations more  approachable, transparent and 
responsive. In this regard, the Ministry and Delhi Police may explore and adopt best 
practices such as establishing dedicated One-Stop Citizen Service Desks at police stations to 
assist visitors with inquiries and reporting proced ures; deploying Community Liaison 
Officers for outreach and follow-up with local comm unities; creating Victim Support 
Corners to provide private and supportive spaces fo r women, children and senior citizens; 

50 
 
instituting structured feedback and grievance redre ssal mechanisms with defined 
timelines; and prominently displaying service chart ers outlining procedures and rights of 
complainants.  
4.8.8 The Committee recommends expansion of mobile and community policing 
initiatives through regular engagement in markets and residential areas, and strengthening 
of online reporting and appointment systems to faci litate access without requiring physical 
visits. Regular training of personnel in communicat ion skills, sensitivity and public 
interface management may also be undertaken, and pe riodic community satisfaction 
surveys may be conducted to assess and improve service delivery. 
4.8.9 The Committee observes that cases relating to  missing persons, particularly women, 
children and vulnerable individuals, have significa nt social and security implications in a 
metropolitan city like Delhi. The Committee is of t he view that a data-driven and research-
oriented approach is necessary to understand the un derlying socio-economic, demographic 
and behavioural factors contributing to such cases.  
The Committee, therefore, recommends that Delhi Pol ice may undertake a 
comprehensive sociological study in collaboration w ith reputed universities and research 
institutions to analyse patterns and root causes of  missing persons’ cases. The study may 
include comparative analysis of missing rates in De lhi vis-à-vis other metropolitan cities in 
India as well as selected international cities, wit h a view to identifying best practices and 
systemic gaps. The findings of such a study may be utilised for policy formulation, 
preventive strategies, improved tracking mechanisms and community-based interventions. 
4.8.10 Further, the Committee observes that effecti ve implementation of provisions 
relating to plea bargaining under the Bharatiya Nag arik Suraksha Sanhita (BNSS) can 
play a significant role in reducing pendency of cas es, expediting disposal of minor offences 
and easing the burden on investigating agencies, co urts and correctional institutions. The 
Committee also observes that structured use of plea  bargaining mechanisms may 
contribute to faster resolution of cases, reduced u ndertrial prisoners and improved 
efficiency of the criminal justice system.  
The Committee, therefore, recommends that Delhi Pol ice may take proactive steps 
to sensitise investigating officers and supervisory  officers regarding the scope and 
procedural requirements of plea bargaining under th e BNSS. Awareness measures may be 
undertaken, in coordination with prosecution and le gal services authorities, to ensure 
informed participation of eligible accused persons.  Periodic review of cases disposed off 
through plea bargaining may also be conducted to as sess its effectiveness in reducing case 
backlog and custodial burden. 
4.8.11 The Committee, further, notes the increasing  deployment of CCTV cameras, body-
worn cameras, traffic surveillance systems, IoT-ena bled devices and integrated 

51 
 
communication platforms by Delhi Police as part of modernization and smart policing 
initiatives. While appreciating the technological a dvancements, the Committee also 
observes that robust data protection and cybersecur ity safeguards are critical to prevent 
misuse, unauthorised access, data breaches and comp romise of sensitive operational 
personal information. The Committee, therefore, rec ommends that Delhi Police may adopt 
a comprehensive data security framework covering en cryption standards, secure data 
storage, controlled access protocols, periodic cybersecurity audits and real-time monitoring 
of surveillance systems. 
4.8.12 The Committee recommends that private indivi duals and establishments installing 
CCTV systems may be encouraged or mandated to procu re devices only from 
manufacturers that comply with notified data securi ty, encryption and secure server 
standards. Periodic advisories and awareness campai gns may also be issued to promote 
adoption of secure systems and prevent misuse, hack ing or unauthorised access to 
surveillance data. 
4.9 TRAFFIC MANAGEMENT IN DELHI 
4.9.1  The Committee sought to know the implementation of modern traffic management 
systems. The Ministry informed that Delhi Traffic P olice is working on the implementation of an 
Intelligent Traffic Management System (ITMS) projec t to improve traffic flow, reduce travel 
time and enhance road safety. The revised Detailed Project Report (DPR Version 4.2) and 
Request for Proposal (RFP Version 2.0) of the proje ct were submitted by C-DAC on 11.07.2025 
and are presently under consideration. As per the r evised DPR, the total estimated cost of the 
ITMS project is ₹2,101.46 crores. Phase-I of the pr oject is proposed to be implemented after one 
year from the date of issuance of work order to the  Master System Integrator (MSI). The project 
includes mobilization and design, supply, installat ion and commissioning along with network 
bandwidth, and testing and go-live operations. 
4.9.2 The Ministry also informed that the ITMS aims  to enhance traffic flow, reduce 
congestion, provide priority movement to emergency vehicles and improve overall commuter 
safety through automated and intelligent interventi ons. In addition, installation and 
commissioning of 203 Red Light Violation Detection (RLVD) cameras at 57 junctions and 125 
Over Speed Violation Detection (OSVD) cameras at 76  locations is under process. It was 
informed that 209 RLVD cameras at 43 junctions and 125 OSVD cameras at 66 locations are 
already operational since 2019–21. The new installa tions are intended to strengthen AI-enabled 
traffic enforcement and automated challan generation. 
4.9.3 It was informed that 62 major congestion poin ts across Delhi have already been identified 
in mid-2025. Focused interventions are being undert aken in coordination with Public Works 
Department (PWD), Municipal Corporation of Delhi (MCD) and other stakeholder agencies. The 
progress made by the concerned agencies / departmen ts in improving road conditions and 
ensuring smoother traffic flow in identified conges ted corridors are evaluated in various high-

52 
 
level meetings. The 62 identified traffic congestio n points across Delhi, includes areas such as 
Bhavbhuti Marg (NDRS), Ajmeri Gate, Shadipur Chowk,  Azadpur Mandi (GTK Road), Ashram 
Chowk, Sarai Kale Khan, Kalindi Kunj, Najafgarh Roa d, Mayapuri Chowk, Sardar Patel Marg, 
Dwarka Palam Flyover, Kapashera Border and Safdarju ng Hospital (Ring Road), among others. 
The Ministry also informed that focused corrective measures are being implemented at these 
locations through coordinated enforcement, infrastr ucture modification and traffic engineering 
solutions. The Committee was apprised that traffic hotspots and chokepoints are identified based 
on the following: 
 Traffic hotspots / chokepoints are identified on the basis of reports / suggestions provided 
by the field functionaries of Delhi Traffic Police deployed across Delhi who regularly 
report where queues, gridlocks, or dangerous situations recur. 
 Citizen feedback channels (like complaints via apps or congestion calls on Helpline No.) 
also inform where persistent problems exist which help Delhi Traffic Police to earmark 
those locations. 
 The Delhi Traffic Police conduct surveys of major roads / junctions to spot places where 
vehicles regularly slow down or get congested. This includes counting traffic volume 
during peak hours and also where road capacity can’t match vehicle flow. 
4.9.4 The Ministry was also enquired about the meas ures to decongest traffic hotspots. The 
Committee was apprised that the following measures are being implemented for decongestion of 
choke points: 
 Upgradation and optimization of traffic signal syst ems through installation of new 
signals and periodic review of signal timings to en sure alignment with prevailing 
traffic volumes and directional flows. 
 Violations that directly affect traffic flow and si gnificantly increase the likelihood of 
road crashes such as driving against the designated  flow of traffic, improper and 
obstructive parking, violation of restricted-entry timings, red-light jumping, over-
speeding, drunken driving, riding without a helmet and other high-risk behaviours are 
accorded on special priority. 
 Joint enforcement and clearance drive with municipa l agencies and local police to 
keep carriageways and footpaths free from encroachments and obstructions. 
 Continuous public engagement through social media p latforms such as X, Facebook, 
WhatsApp and others for timely dissemination of tra ffic advisories, diversions and 
updates. 
 Close and continuous coordination with road-owning and civic agencies is made to 
facilitate timely infrastructure improvements and i mplement engineering 
interventions critical for smooth traffic flow and enhanced road safety. 

53 
 
 Vehicle based enforcement and area-based enforcemen t drives are also undertaken in 
addition to routine enforcement. 
Observation/ Recommendation 
4.9.5 The Committee notes that 62 major traffic con gestion points have been identified 
across Delhi and corrective measures are being unde rtaken in coordination with civic 
agencies. The Committee recommends that a time-boun d action plan with clearly defined 
responsibilities for each stakeholder agency may be  prepared for all identified hotspots. 
Periodic public reporting of progress and measurabl e indicators such as reduction in 
average travel time, queue length and accident rate s may be introduced to assess 
effectiveness of interventions. A long-term urban m obility perspective, including 
coordination with urban planning authorities, may a lso be integrated into traffic 
management strategies to ensure sustainable deconge stion and a system of traffic flying 
squads may be put in place so that the same may be deployed at designated points during 
peak hours. 
4.9.6 The Committee observes that unregulated parki ng around office complexes, courts, 
hospitals and commercial establishments significant ly contributes to congestion and 
obstruction of roads. The Committee recommends that  Delhi Police, in coordination with 
Municipal and Urban Development authorities, may un dertake a comprehensive parking 
management strategy, including strict enforcement a gainst illegal parking on roads and 
residential areas, promotion of designated parking zones and use of technology-enabled 
parking monitoring systems. The Committee further r ecommends that commercial 
establishments may be mandated to ensure adequate o ff-street parking facilities to reduce 
spill-over onto public roads. 
4.9.7 The Committee notes with concern the increasi ng instances of unnecessary honking 
in the National Capital, leading to noise pollution  and public inconvenience, particularly in 
residential areas and near schools, hospitals and c ourts. The Committee recommends that 
Delhi Police may intensify enforcement of existing provisions relating to use of horns and 
pressure horns, especially in notified “No-Horn Zon es”. The Committee further 
recommends that sustained sensitisation and public awareness campaigns may be 
undertaken to discourage habitual and non-essential  honking, and that technology-enabled 
enforcement measures may be explored to ensure comp liance and promote responsible 
road behaviour. 
4.9.8 The Committee also notes instances of motorcy cles fitted with modified or non-
standard silencers that produce excessive noise, le ading to sound pollution and causing 
disturbance and unease to commuters and residents. The Committee recommends that 
Delhi Police may strengthen enforcement against suc h violations through regular checking 
drives, strict penalties and coordination with tran sport authorities to ensure compliance 
with prescribed vehicle noise standards.  

54 
 
4.9.9 The Committee observes that begging at traffi c signals not only poses safety risks 
but also disrupts traffic flow. The Committee recom mends that Delhi Police may work in 
coordination with Social Welfare Departments and Ci vil Society Organisations to address 
this issue through a humane and rehabilitative appr oach. Sustained enforcement at major 
intersections may be combined with rehabilitation, counselling and livelihood support 
initiatives to address the root causes of the problem.

and inter-agency coordination as mandated under the  NDPS Act (Narcotics Drugs Psychotropic 
Substances Act, 1985). It will also support improve d investigation processes, evidence handling 

55 
 
and digital forensic capabilities, thereby strength ening prosecution outcomes and improving 
conviction rates. The Ministry stated that the allo cation represents a focused capacity-building 
measure aligned with the National Drug Control Stra tegy and the Government’s zero-tolerance 
approach towards narcotics. 
4.10.4 The Committee was further informed that the mandate of the NCB includes coordination 
of enforcement actions of various Central and State  authorities; coordination of action with the 
Ministry of Health & Family Welfare, Ministry of So cial Justice & Empowerment and other

international organisations. With regard to future plans, the Ministry informed that emphasis is 
being placed on enhancement of technical capabiliti es and expansion of pan-India presence. 
Creation of six new Zones at Shimla, Port Blair, Di mapur, Aizawl, Gangtok and Shillong has 
been proposed to strengthen regional enforcement capacity and improve operational reach.

Observation/ Recommendation 
4.10.14 The Committee notes that narcotics traffick ing routes are increasingly 
exploiting vulnerable stretches along land and coas tal borders. Keeping in view of the 
transnational nature of drug trafficking and its li nkage with organized crime and internal 
security concerns, the Committee recommends that th e Narcotics Control Bureau (NCB) 
may intensify coordination with police forces of bo rder States and with other border 
guarding agencies to ensure sustained area dominati on and intelligence-based interdiction 
in vulnerable border districts. The Committee, furt her, recommends that joint task forces, 
real-time intelligence sharing mechanisms and coord inated field operations may be 
incorporated to disrupt supply chains of narcotic d rugs and psychotropic substances at the 
source and transit points. 
 
4.10.15 The Committee observes that frontline enfor cement officers engaged in high-
risk anti-narcotics operations deserve appropriate recognition and motivation. The 
Committee recommends that a structured and transpar ent reward mechanism may be 
strengthened for officers and teams involved in sig nificant seizures of narcotic drugs and 
psychotropic substances, subject to safeguards and accountability norms. Such incentives 
may serve to enhance morale, encourage proactive en forcement and reinforce the 
Government’s zero-tolerance approach towards drug trafficking. 
4.10.16 The Committee observes that against a sanct ioned strength of 1,901 posts, 
379 posts are presently vacant in National Investigation Agency (NIA). The Committee is of 
the view that such vacancies may impact operational  capacity, investigation timelines and 
field-level enforcement effectiveness. The Committe e, therefore, recommends that the 
Ministry may take expeditious steps to fill up vaca nt posts through direct recruitment, 
deputation and promotion, as applicable, within a d efined timeframe. The Committee 
further recommends that manpower planning may be al igned with the proposed expansion 
of zonal presence to ensure adequate staffing in newly created zones . 

58 
 
4.10.17 The Committee notes that BPR&D has undertak en training initiatives for 
officers across the five pillars of the Criminal Ju stice System on the Nyaya Sanhitayein. 
Considering the transformative nature of the Bharat iya Nyaya Sanhita (BNS), Bharatiya 
Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita (BNSS) and Bharatiya Saksh ya Adhiniyam (BSA), the 
Committee is of the view that sustained and structu red capacity building of police 
personnel at all levels is essential for effective implementation.  
The Committee, therefore, recommends that BPR&D may  further scale up and 
institutionalise specialised training programmes on  the new criminal laws, with particular 
emphasis on investigation procedures, evidence coll ection, digital documentation, forensic 
integration and trial processes under the revised l egal framework. Refresher courses, 
scenario-based simulations and field-level practica l modules may also be incorporated to 
ensure uniform understanding and application across States and Union Territories.  
In view of the rapidly evolving nature of crime, pa rticularly cyber-enabled and 
technology-driven offences, the Committee further r ecommends that dedicated courses on 
Artificial Intelligence (AI), Big Data Analytics, C ybercrime Investigation, Digital Forensics 
and Technology-driven Policing may be introduced an d mainstreamed in police training 
curricula. Advanced certification programmes and pa rtnerships with technical institutions 
may also be explored to equip officers with analyti cal and technological competencies 
required for modern policing. Periodic evaluation o f training outcomes may be undertaken 
to assess impact and identify emerging skill gaps.

4.11.1 The Ministry informed that the provisions un der Revenue and Capital heads cater to the 
establishment related expenditure needs of the Cent ral Forensic Science Laboratories (CFSL) 
and the Directorate of Forensic Sciences (DFS). Fro m BE 2026-27 onwards the budget of DFS 
and CFSL has been combined under DFS. These agencie s provide forensic investigative reports 
to various crime investigative agencies, police for ces, etc. The budget provision caters to the 
administrative expenses of the organizations. 
4.11.2 The Committee noted that allocation under th is head has increased from ₹99.57 crores in 
RE 2025–26 to ₹132.89 crores in BE 2026–27. The Min istry informed that presently 07 Central 
Forensic Science Laboratories (CFSLs) are operation al in the country. In addition, 08 new 

59 
 
CFSLs are proposed to be established at Odisha, Chh attisgarh, Tamil Nadu, Rajasthan, Bihar, 
Uttar Pradesh, Kerala and Jammu & Kashmir to augment forensic capacity. 
4.11.3 With regard to shortages of trained forensic  manpower, the Ministry informed that 
several measures are being undertaken. These includ e establishment of new campuses of the 
National Forensic Sciences University (NFSU) to mee t long-term manpower requirements; 
amendment of Recruitment Rules to incorporate addit ional and relevant qualifications for 
forensic experts; and expeditious filling of vacant  posts. The National Forensic Sciences 
University (NFSU), along with the Directorate of Fo rensic Science Services (DFSS), conducts 
the Forensic Aptitude and Caliber Test (FACT/FACT P lus) examinations. Qualified candidates 
are engaged in Central and State Forensic Science L aboratories on a contractual basis to bridge 
immediate manpower gaps. DFSS also organises regula r training programmes to upgrade and 
enhance the skills of forensic experts working in Central and State FSLs. 
4.11.4 The Committee was informed that forensic lab oratories across the country have been 
equipped with modern technologies such as automated DNA extraction systems, Real-Time PCR 
systems, Thermal Cyclers, DNA Sequencers, advanced chromatography systems, cyber and 
digital forensic tools, and modern data analysis an d case management software. Under the 
Scheme for Modernization of Forensic Capacities (SM FC) and Nirbhaya Fund schemes, funds 
are being utilised for procurement of state-of-the- art forensic equipment, modernization of 
laboratory infrastructure, establishment and upgradation of DNA and cyber forensic laboratories, 
and capacity building of forensic personnel. 
4.11.5 Under the Umbrella Scheme “Safety for Women” , a National Forensic Data Centre 
(NFDC) has been approved with a financial outlay of  ₹200.16 crores, to be established at CFSL 
Bhopal. Further, establishment of six National Cybe r Forensic Laboratories (NCFLs) at CFSLs 
Chandigarh, Delhi, Kolkata, Pune, Bhopal and Kamrup  has been approved with a total outlay of 
₹126.84 crores. The NCFLs at Delhi and Kamrup are f ully operational and have commenced 
examination of cases, while the remaining laboratories are under development. 
Observation/ Recommendation 
4.11.6 The Committee notes the increase in allocati on from ₹99.57 crores at RE 2025–26 to 
₹132.89 crores at BE 2026–27 and the proposal to es tablish eight new Central Forensic 
Science Laboratories (CFSLs) in addition to the sev en currently operational laboratories. 
Considering the growing volume and complexity of ca ses, particularly under the new 
criminal laws mandating greater reliance on scienti fic evidence, the Committee 
recommends that establishment and operationalisatio n of the proposed CFSLs may be 
undertaken in a time-bound manner. The infrastructu re creation may be accompanied by 
adequate manpower deployment and equipment provisio ning to ensure that the 
laboratories become fully functional without delay.  The recruitment to sanctioned posts 
may be expedited to ensure long-term institutional capacity. The Ministry may create a 

60 
 
structured cadre management and career progression framework to attract and retain 
qualified forensic professionals.  
4.11.7 The Committee appreciates the technological upgradation of laboratories with 
advanced DNA systems, cyber forensic tools and mode rn data analysis software. In view of 
increasing digital and cyber-enabled crimes, the Co mmittee recommends that forensic 
laboratories may be fully integrated with investiga tion and prosecution systems through 
secure digital platforms to ensure seamless evidenc e transfer and timely reporting. The 
operationalisation of the National Forensic Data Ce ntre (NFDC) and remaining National 
Cyber Forensic Laboratories (NCFLs) may be closely monitored to maximise their utility 
in handling cyber and electronic evidence. The incr eased financial allocation should 
translate into measurable reduction in forensic pen dency and faster turnaround time for 
reports. Performance benchmarks such as average rep ort submission timelines, case 
disposal rates and utilisation of modern equipment may be periodically reviewed. Special 
emphasis may be laid on strengthening DNA and cyber forensic capabilities in view of their 
critical role in securing higher conviction rates.

4.12.3 The Committee sought details regarding measu res undertaken to strengthen civil defence 
and resilient infrastructure in border districts of Jammu & Kashmir. The Ministry informed that a 
total of 9,663 bunkers have been constructed in bor der areas of Jammu & Kashmir at a cost of 
₹357.40 crores to provide protection to civilians d uring cross-border shelling. In addition, 
compensation is being provided for damage to houses  and property caused by cross-border 
firing/shelling as per extant norms. Ex-gratia relief is also provided in cases of death or disability  
resulting from cross-border firing/shelling in acco rdance with approved relief and rehabilitation 
guidelines. 
4.12.4 The Committee was informed that Border Guard ing Forces face complex operational 
challenges arising from rugged mountains, dense forests, riverine and marshy stretches, unfenced 
gaps, habitation near the zero line and adverse wea ther conditions such as fog, heavy rainfall and 
extreme temperatures. These factors restrict visibi lity, mobility and communication, while 
porous landscapes facilitate infiltration, smugglin g and other illegal activities. Limited road 
connectivity, absence of lateral roads, power short ages, restricted construction windows and 
dependence on air/porter logistics further constrai n operations. Large unfenced frontages require 
simultaneous static manning, long-range patrols, mo bile domination and counter-insurgency 
tasks, resulting in manpower strain. Terrain maskin g and adverse weather conditions also reduce 
the effectiveness of technological systems, and per sonnel face health challenges in remote and 
high-risk environments. The Status of Border Infrastructure is as follows:-

4.12.5 The Ministry further stated that to address the challenges faced by Border Guarding 
Forces, layered deployment strategies and vulnerabi lity mapping are undertaken to strengthen 
surveillance and operational dominance. Measures in clude round-the-clock patrolling, nakas and 
observation posts; deployment of thermal imagers, n ight vision devices, radars, UAVs and 
integrated electronic systems; erection and mainten ance of border fencing and temporary 
barricades; illumination of vulnerable patches; and  use of boats, floating BOPs and specialized 
vehicles in riverine areas. Infrastructure augmenta tion through helipads, solar power installations 
and communication upgradation is also being underta ken. These efforts are supported by 
intelligence strengthening, inter-agency coordination and community engagement. 
4.12.6 The Committee was also informed that during the last five years, 4,323 cases of drone 
sightings were reported along international borders . However, 967 cases of drone incursions 
occurred, which were intercepted or neutralized. Th ese operations resulted in seizure of 710 
ammunitions, 75 arms and 641 kg of drugs. 
4.12.7 The Committee was apprised that unmapped, un demarcated, forested and riverine 
stretches are secured through continuous surveillance and physical domination. Measures include 
deployment at Border Out Posts (BOPs), regular patr olling of ingress routes, Temporary 
Operating Bases, use of Night Vision Devices, Hand Held Thermal Imagers, Long Range 
Reconnaissance and Observation Systems, satellite i magery, watch towers, UAVs and smart 
fencing pilots. Specialized measures for riverine a reas include patrolling with boats and 
watercraft, floating posts, surveillance of landing  points, installation of thermal imaging systems 
and integrated electronic surveillance such as PTZ cameras, radars and intrusion detection 

63 
 
sensors. Forest and depth areas are monitored throu gh additional manpower deployment, motion 
sensors, infrared alarms and drones. Border fencing , floodlighting and temporary or solar 
illumination of dark patches further enhance securi ty. Inter-agency coordination and community 
engagement are integral components of this strategy. 
4.12.8 The Committee was informed that along the In do-Myanmar border, challenges such as 
illegal migration, trafficking and insurgent activities are being addressed through intelligence-led 
operations, enhanced surveillance and coordinated g round measures. Coordination with local 
communities, Myanmar Army and associated security a gencies is maintained. A joint counter-
drone mechanism with monthly reporting has also bee n instituted. Along the Indo-Bangladesh 
border, detailed vulnerability mapping, augmented m anpower deployment and round-the-clock 
patrolling are undertaken by the Border Security Fo rce (BSF). Surveillance is strengthened 
through use of Hand Held Thermal Imagers (HHTIs), N ight Vision Devices (NVDs), UAVs, 
integrated CCTV/PTZ systems, IR sensors and floodli ghting. Riverine domination is maintained 
through boats and floating BOPs. Anti-Human Traffic king Units operate in coordination with 
local Police, Railway Police (GRP) and other agenci es. Joint operations are conducted with local 
Police, NCB, DRI, Customs and Border Guard Banglade sh (BGB) to address illegal migration, 
trafficking and other cross-border crimes. 
4.12.9 The Committee was further informed that the Border Area Development Programme 
(BADP) functions as a gap-funding scheme aimed at a ddressing critical infrastructure 
deficiencies not covered under any regular scheme o r any development by other ministries. 
However, BADP is presently in its sunset phase and no new projects are being approved. Funds 
are being released only towards committed liabilities for ongoing works up to FY 2025–26. 
Observation/Recommendation  
4.12.10 The Committee notes the substantial capital  allocation under Border 
Security in BE 2026–27 and observes that while prog ress has been achieved in fencing, 
floodlighting and construction of Border Out Posts (BOPs) along several borders, certain 
components, particularly roads along the Indo–Pakis tan and Indo–Myanmar borders, 
show comparatively slower completion. The Committee recommends that the Ministry may 
prepare a time-bound completion roadmap for pending  road and infrastructure 
components, especially in strategically sensitive s ectors, and furnish periodic progress 
updates.  
4.12.11 The Committee observes that operational cha llenges such as difficult terrain, 
riverine stretches, adverse weather conditions and unfenced gaps continue to constrain 
effective border management. The Committee recommen ds that infrastructure 
development, particularly lateral connectivity, hel ipad support, communication 
upgradation and renewable energy-based power supply , may be prioritised in remote and 
high-risk areas to enhance mobility and operational efficiency of Border Guarding Forces. 

64 
 
4.12.12 The Committee takes note of the constructio n of 9,663 bunkers in border 
districts of Jammu & Kashmir and provision of compe nsation and ex-gratia relief to 
affected civilians. The Committee recommends that p eriodic assessment of civil defence 
preparedness in border villages be undertaken to en sure adequacy of protective 
infrastructure and timely disbursal of relief assistance. 
4.12.13  The Committee observes the increasing inci dence of drone sightings and 
confirmed incursions along international borders an d the seizures made pursuant thereto. 
The Committee recommends that counter-drone capabil ities, including detection, 
neutralization and forensic analysis, be further st rengthened through induction of 
advanced technologies and enhanced inter-agency coordination mechanisms. 
4.12.14 The Committee observes that illegal migrati on, trafficking and trans-border 
crimes remain areas of concern along the Indo–Myanm ar and Indo–Bangladesh borders. 
The Committee recommends strengthening coordinated intelligence-sharing frameworks 
with State Police and central agencies and enhancin g capacity of Anti-Human Trafficking 
Units in border districts.

4.14 INTER-OPERABLE CRIMINAL JUSTICE SYSTEM (ICJS) 
 
4.14.1 This Provision is for a project on Inter-Ope rable Criminal Justice System (ICJS). The 
project involves enhancement in the criminal justic e IT systems for a paper-less operation, 
interlinking the IT systems for seamless data excha nge and also to provide for new technologies 
for smart policing. 
The allocation made for Inter-Operable Criminal Justice System  
Table No. 4.14  
(amount in crores of ₹) 
 Actuals 2024-25 BE 2025-26 RE 2025-26 BE 2026-27 
Revenue 108.62  300.20  300.01  550.00  
4.14.2 The Committee noted that allocation under th e Inter-Operable Criminal Justice System 
(ICJS) has increased from ₹300.01 crores in RE 2025 –26 to ₹550.00 crores in BE 2026–27. The 
Ministry informed that the enhanced allocation will  be utilized for implementation of ICJS 2.0 
across all States and Union Territories. State Acti on Plans covering hardware procurement, 
network connectivity, capacity building and Informa tion, Education and Communication (IEC) 
activities have been approved as per ICJS guideline s. The allocation will meet procurement and 
implementation requirements of States/UTs and also cover committed liabilities relating to 
operation, maintenance and enhancement of core appl ications such as Crime and Criminal 
Tracking Network and Systems (CCTNS), e-Courts (CrPI) and the ICJS integration framework. 
4.14.3 The Ministry further informed that increased  allocation will be utilized under ICJS 2.0 to 
strengthen digital infrastructure and enable full-s cale integration of CCTNS with e-Courts, e-
Forensics and e-Prisons through the ICJS mediation layer. This integration is intended to 
facilitate secure and structured data exchange amon g criminal justice stakeholders. The Ministry 
stated that upon full operationalisation across all  pillars, the system is expected to support 
adherence to statutory timelines prescribed under t he Naveen Nyaya Sanhitas and improve 
overall process efficiency in investigation, prosecution and trial stages. 
4.14.4 The allocation for FY 2026–27 will also supp ort development, deployment and 
nationwide rollout of applications such as e-Summon s, e-Sakshya and Nyaya Shruti, along with 
the required supporting infrastructure and training initiatives for stakeholders. 
 
Observation / Recommendation 

68 
 
4.14.5 The Committee observes that while ICJS 2.0 a ims to enhance interoperability and 
transparency among criminal justice stakeholders, the victim remains largely dependent on 
intermediaries for information regarding progress o f his/her case. In order to strengthen 
victim-centric justice delivery, the Committee reco mmends that a secure digital interface 
may be developed enabling victims to access limited  case status information, such as stage 
of investigation, filing of charge sheet, court lis ting and service of summons. Such access 
may be provided through authenticated identity veri fication mechanisms, including 
Aadhaar-based or PAN-based verification, subject to  compliance with applicable data 
protection and privacy safeguards. The access proto cols must be carefully designed to 
prevent disclosure of sensitive investigative mater ial, protect witness confidentiality and 
ensure integrity of ongoing proceedings. This measure would enhance transparency, reduce 
procedural uncertainty for victims and strengthen p ublic confidence in the justice delivery 
system.  
4.15 MODERNIZATION OF PRISONS  
4.15.1 The Committee noted that allocation under th e Modernization of Prisons Project has been 
restored to ₹300.00 crores in BE 2026–27 after redu ction at the RE 2025–26 stage. The Ministry 
informed that the allocation for FY 2026–27 will be  utilised for completion of High Security 
Prisons (HSPs) in Assam, Jharkhand, Karnataka, Punj ab, Tamil Nadu and NCT of Delhi; 
procurement of prison security equipment such as Do or Frame Metal Detectors, baggage 
scanners and frisking equipment; upgradation of vid eo-conferencing infrastructure; and 
implementation of correctional programmes, including vocational training for inmates. 
4.15.2 The Ministry informed that the allocation wa s reduced in RE 2025-26 because progress 
of the Modernization of Prisons Project was affecte d in some States/UTs due to various reasons 
such as delay in finalisation of tender due to court case in Karnataka, site-related constraints such 
as low-lying land requiring land filling work in As sam and delay in finalisation of tender in NCT 
of Delhi. These issues have now been addressed and construction work has started in Assam, 
Karnataka and NCT of Delhi has also completed the t ender process and issued work order on 
05.02.2026. The Project, particularly the establish ment of High Security Prisons, is of national 
importance as it strengthens secure custody of hard ened and high-risk offenders and supports the 
broader internal security framework. Accordingly, a dequate financial provision will be required 
in FY 2026-27 for completion of the construction wo rk of the HSPs in 6 States/UT and 
upgradation of security infrastructure in prisons a cross the country. The outcomes of the Project 
are being monitored through regular review of physi cal and financial progress by States/UTs. 
Progress of High Security Prison work, deployment of security equipment and implementation of 
correctional programmes are the key indicators for assessing improvements in prison safety, 
infrastructure and rehabilitation. 
4.15.3 The Committee was also apprised that while t he Modernization of Prisons Project does 
not provide for upgradation of existing prisons or establishment of open jails, it contributes to 
better prison management through strengthening of s ecurity infrastructure and correctional 

69 
 
administration. The Model Prisons and Correctional Services Act is a prison reform initiative and 
States and UTs are being continuously encouraged to  adopt the Model Act in their respective 
jurisdictions. The allocation under the Project wil l therefore be utilised on completion of High 
Security Prisons and organisation of correctional p rogrammes, including vocational training for 
inmates, aimed at improving prison security, management and rehabilitation outcomes. 
Observation / Recommendation 
4.15.4 
 The Committee notes that the Modernization of Priso ns Project primarily focuses 
on establishment of High Security Prisons and stren gthening of security infrastructure. 
While secure custody of high-risk offenders is esse ntial, the Committee observes that issues 
relating to overcrowding, ageing infrastructure and  living conditions in existing prisons 
remain areas of concern.  
The Committee recommends that the Ministry may exam ine the feasibility of 
extending modernization support to upgradation of e xisting prison infrastructure, 
including improvement of sanitation, healthcare fac ilities, digital record management and 
segregation facilities, in consultation with States /UTs. A balanced approach addressing 
both security and humane custodial conditions would  strengthen overall prison 
administration and internal security objectives. 
4.15.5 The Committee notes that a substantial propo rtion of the prison population 
comprises undertrial prisoners. The Committee, ther efore, recommends that appropriate 
segregation between undertrial and convicted prison ers may be ensured in prisons, in 
accordance with established legal and correctional principles. While undertaking prison 
modernization, States/UTs may consider prioritising infrastructure planning that facilitates 
separate accommodation and suitable custodial arran gements for these categories so that 
such measures would help safeguard the rights of un dertrial prisoners, reduce the risk of 
negative influence of convicted criminals and contr ibute to better prison management and 
discipline. 
4.16 SUPPORT TO POOR PRISONERS 
4.16.1 The Committee sought details regarding the n umber of prisoners benefitted under the 
Support to Poor Prisoners Scheme, the average proce ssing time for assistance and challenges 
encountered in timely delivery of support. The Mini stry informed that since inception of the 
Scheme, 262 prisoners have been provided financial assistance. It was further stated that the 
Guidelines and Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) o f the Scheme have recently been revised 
to ensure time-bound and effective implementation. 
4.16.2 As per the revised SOP, cases of undertrial prisoners who are not released within seven 
days of grant of bail are required to be reported b y Jail Authorities to the concerned District 
Legal Services Authority (DLSA). Verification and i nteraction by Jail Visiting 

70 
 
Lawyers/Paralegal Volunteers is to be completed wit hin five days. Thereafter, the cases are 
placed before the District Level Empowered Committe e, which is mandated to meet twice a 
month (on the 1st and 3rd Mondays of each month or the next working day) to consider eligible 
cases. The Committee is required to take a decision  and ensure availability of funds to the Court 
within a further five days. The Ministry stated tha t these clearly defined timelines are intended to 
minimise delays and procedural bottlenecks in extending assistance. 
4.16.3 With regard to challenges in timely delivery  of support, the Ministry informed that 
several States/UTs have reported that a significant  number of prisoners are not eligible under the 
Scheme due to their involvement in multiple cases o r in heinous offences such as terrorism-
related offences, offences affecting national secur ity, dowry death, rape, human trafficking and 
offences under the POCSO Act, NDPS Act, Unlawful Ac tivities (Prevention) Act, Prevention of 
Corruption Act and Prevention of Money Laundering A ct, for which benefits under the Scheme 
are not admissible. 
Observation / Recommendation 
 
4.16.4 The Committee notes that while the Support t o Poor Prisoners Scheme provides 
important financial assistance to undertrial prison ers who are unable to furnish bail, the 
number of beneficiaries since inception remains lim ited. The Committee appreciates the 
revision of Guidelines and Standard Operating Proce dure to ensure defined timelines at 
each stage of processing. The Committee recommends that greater awareness regarding 
the Scheme may be generated among prison authoritie s, legal aid institutions and eligible 
prisoners to ensure that deserving beneficiaries are not left out due to lack of information. 
 
4.17 VIBRANT VILLAGES PROGRAMME 
4.17.1 The Ministry has informed the Committee that  Vibrant Village Programme is for 
comprehensive development in phased manner of selec ted border villages. This includes village 
infrastructure development, road connectivity and o ther developmental works. The Ministry also 
informed that the Vibrant Villages Programme-II foc uses on improving living conditions and 
livelihoods in selected border villages. Its core a reas include village infrastructure, road and 
telecom connectivity, electrification, basic servic es, livelihood support, and integration of border 
populations with the nation. 
Vibrant Villages Programme–I 
4.17.2 The Committee was informed that the Vibrant Villages Programme (VVP) was launched 
with the objective of enhancing vibrancy in border villages and reversing outmigration by 
providing adequate incentives and improving living conditions. The Programme covers 662 
villages across 4 States and 1 Union Territory, wit h an approximate population coverage of 1.5 
lakh persons. The financial outlay for FY 2023–26 i s ₹4,800 crores, including the road 

71 
 
component, with an additional allocation of ₹440.82  crores for construction of Long Span 
Bridges (LSBs). 
4.17.3 The Ministry informed that since its launch in Kibithoo, Anjaw district, Arunachal 
Pradesh in 2023, several outreach and development a ctivities have been undertaken. Seventeen 
Union Cabinet Ministers have visited 34 VVP villages across 8 districts, and 92 officials from 17 
Ministries have visited 259 villages. More than 8,5 00 activities have been organized in the 
identified villages. Under VVP-I, 263 projects amounting to ₹116 crore have been sanctioned for 
development of tourism infrastructure. In addition,  112 road works amounting to ₹2,513 crore 
have been approved. A total of 2,663 projects worth  ₹1,172 crore have been sanctioned by the 
Ministry of Home Affairs under VVP-I and through co nvergence with Central Ministries and 
Departments for comprehensive development of these villages. The Programme aims to 
strengthen socio-economic resilience, prevent borde r depopulation and reinforce the first line of 
defence through community development initiatives. 
Vibrant Villages Programme–II (VVP-II) 
4.17.4 The Committee was further informed that Vibr ant Villages Programme–II has been 
approved as a Central Sector Scheme with effect from April 2025, with a total financial outlay of 
₹6,839 crores for the period FY 2024–29. VVP-II cov ers 1,954 villages across approximately 
100 districts in 17 States and Union Territories lo cated along land borders other than the 
Northern Border already covered under VVP-I. 
4.17.5 The objectives of VVP-II include strengtheni ng internal security by assimilating border 
populations with the national mainstream and encour aging their role as the “eyes and ears” of 
border guarding forces; improving living conditions  and livelihood opportunities; and weaning 
communities away from inimical activities while controlling trans-border crime. 
4.17.6 The strategy under VVP-II includes saturatio n of villages abutting International Land 
Borders (ILBs) with connectivity infrastructure suc h as telecom, roads, power and television; 
creation of diverse and sustainable livelihood oppo rtunities; development of infrastructure 
including education and tourism; and undertaking bo rder-specific outreach activities. The 
Programme seeks to enhance socio-economic developme nt, improve connectivity and 
infrastructure and reinforce community participatio n in national security objectives in border 
areas. 
Observation / Recommendation 
4.17.7 The Committee appreciates the Vibrant Villag es Programme (VVP) as a significant 
initiative aimed at comprehensive development of bo rder villages through phased 
infrastructure augmentation, livelihood generation and enhanced connectivity. The 
Committee notes that VVP-I has led to substantial p rogress in road connectivity, tourism 
infrastructure and convergence-based developmental activities and that VVP-II expands 
the scope of coverage to additional border regions.  The Programme’s emphasis on socio-

72 
 
economic resilience, prevention of outmigration and  integration of border populations with 
the national mainstream is commendable and aligns w ith broader national security 
objectives. The Committee recommends that livelihoo d initiatives under VVP-II be closely 
aligned with local geographic and cultural strength s, including promotion of border 
tourism, agro-based industries, handicrafts and eco -tourism. Skill development 
programmes tailored to local conditions may be inte grated with market linkages to ensure 
sustainable income generation. Enhanced coordinatio n with Border Guarding Forces and 
community institutions may also be incorporated to strengthen participatory security and 
foster a sense of shared responsibility in border management. 
4.18 MODERNIZATION OF POLICE FORCES (MoPF)  
 
4.18.1 The Ministry informed that the Scheme Modern ization of Police Forces seeks to bring 
under its fold many smaller schemes primarily aimed  at facilitating and assisting the various 
State Police Forces, both in terms of finances and infrastructure up-gradation, in their efforts in 
combating various kinds of criminal activities, cou nter-insurgency operations, etc.The provision 
for the Umbrella Scheme ‘Modernization of Police Fo rces of States and UTs comprises 
allocations for the following sub-schemes/components: 
 Assistance to States for Modernization of Police; 
 Assistance to States for Special Projects /Programmes for upgrading police infrastructure; 
 Assistance to Central Agencies for LWE Management; 
 Civic Action Plan (CAP); 
 Media Plan (Advertising and Publicity); 
 Special Central Assistance (SCA) to 35 worst affected LWE districts; 
 Security Related Expenditure (SRE) [LWE – Central and State Sectors]; 
 SRE (J&K): Relief and Rehabilitation; 
 SRE (J&K): Security Environment; 
 SRE (J&K): Police; 
 SRE (NE); 
 Special Infrastructure Scheme (SIS) along with cons truction of 250 fortified Police    
Stations in LWE affected States; and 
 Reimbursement to States for administration of Central Acts and Regulations 
 Modernization of Forensic Capacities- to develop op erationally independent high quality 
forensic sciences facilities in states/Union Territ ories, for aiding scientific and timely 
investigation 
 
Observation/ Recommendation 
 
4.18.2 The Committee recommends that modernization initiatives be aligned with 
emerging security challenges such as cybercrime, fi nancial fraud and transnational 

73 
 
organised crime, so that police forces are equipped  not only for traditional law and order 
duties but also for technology-driven and intellige nce-led policing in the evolving security 
environment.

(Demand No. 52), Chandigarh (Demand No.53), Dadra a nd Nagar Haveli and Daman and Diu

three Grants relate to UTs with Legislatures; Trans fers to Delhi (Demand No. 57), Transfers to 
Jammu & Kashmir (Demand No.58) and Transfers to Pud ucherry (Demand No.59). The needs of 
UTs are fulfilled in the Union Budget. The overall Budgetary provisions for the years 2025-26

5.1.3 The Committee notes, from the perusal of the overall budgetary provisions and 
expenditure provided by the Ministry that most of t he Union Territories have performed 
well with regard to the utilization of funds agains t allocation made in RE 2025-26. 
Although the funds utilisation in Andaman and Nicob ar Islands, Dadra and Nagar Haveli 
and Daman and Diu and Lakshadweep is comparatively lower, the UTs of Andaman and 
Nicobar Islands and Daman and Diu and Dadra and Nag ar Haveli have been able to utilize 
approximately 70% and 77% respectively of the alloc ated funds against allocation made in 
RE 2025-26. The Committee appreciates the efforts o f the UT administrations for 
efficiently utilising their allocated funds.

5.1.4 The Committee notes that the allocations for key sectors in these Union Territories 
have been enhanced in BE 2026-27 over the previous year. The Committee recommends 
that these budgetary provisions may translate into timely implementation of schemes and 
projects and that regular monitoring may be underta ken so as to ensure full and efficient 
utilisation of the allocated funds within the financial year. 
 
5.2 DEMAND NO. 52- ANDAMAN AND NICOBAR ISLANDS

augmentation, disaster preparedness and essential public services. 
5.2.2 In its replies, the Ministry has informed the  Committee that the priority sectors/projects 
identified for implementation during 2026-27 includ e augmentation of shipping and port 
infrastructure; construction of classrooms in vario us schools; strengthening and retrofitting of 
Chatham Bridge; procurement of buses, construction of a modern bus terminus and transit 
accommodation; construction of anganwadi buildings;  supply, installation and commissioning 
of automated tsunami sirens; works under the urban and rural water supply schemes; purchase 
of HSD, POL, etc.; purchase of power; provision of subsidy under various schemes; and 
construction, repair, maintenance and improvement o f State Highways, rural roads and district 
roads, including release of grants under the Roads & Bridges scheme.

Observation/ Recommendation 
 
5.2.4 
 The Committee notes the UT Administration’s focus o n improvement of rural 
roads, procurement of buses, maintenance and streng thening of ports and shipping 
infrastructure, drinking water supply, power genera tion, health facilities and development 
of school infrastructure. These schemes are importa nt for the infrastructural development 
of the UT and making life easier for the local popu lation. The Committee observes that 
allocation of budget is the first step towards impl ementation of a scheme, but without 
regular and periodic monitoring of the projects sta tus, the budget can remain 
underutilized. The Committee recommends that the UT  administration may ensure timely 
implementation of the schemes/projects included in the BE 2026-27 and the utilization of 
entire allocation within the Financial year. 
 
AIR CONNECTIVITY 
 
5.2.5  Andaman and Nicobar Islands constitute the l argest archipelago system in the Bay of 
Bengal and are located thousands of kilometres away  from the Indian mainland. The UT is 
connected to the mainland primarily through air and  water routes. The Committee took note of 
the increased tourism activity in the region and en quired about the steps taken to augment air 

78 
 
connectivity, including the enhancement of airport capacity in Port Blair and proposals for 
establishing additional airports to connect remote islands of the UT with the mainland. 
 
5.2.6 The Ministry informed the Committee that a Ne w Integrated Terminal Building (NITB) 
at Veer Savarkar International Airport, Sri Vijaya Puram, has been made operational since 
August 2023 with an annual handling capacity of 50 lakh passengers. To meet future demand, an 
alternate Greenfield Airport at Sri Vijaya Puram ha s been planned. The Committee appreciated 
this operationalisation, noting that the new termin al has enhanced passenger-handling capacity 
significantly.  
 
5.2.7 The Committee was further informed helicopter  services are operational from Sri Vijaya 
Puram to Car Nicobar, Diglipur, Mayabunder, Rangat,  Kamorta, Katchal, Chowra, Teressa, 
Shaheed Dweep, Swaraj Dweep and Campbell Bay. Under  the Ude Desh ka Aam Nagrik 
(UDAN) scheme, the UT has also planned connectivity  to Diglipur, Car Nicobar and Campbell 
Bay through 19-seater fixed-wing aircraft. Seaplane services under the UDAN scheme have been 
approved and are under consideration for operationa lisation, with a view to improving last-mile 
connectivity to the remote islands. 
 
Observation/ Recommendation 
 
5.2.8 The Committee also deliberated upon the conce rns raised by Members regarding 
high airfares to the UT and the limited availabilit y of commercial seats. The Committee 
recommends that larger aircraft may be deployed on these routes to increase passenger 
capacity and help reduce airfares. Early operationa lisation of the approved seaplane 
services under the UDAN scheme may be ensured to im prove last-mile connectivity to 
remote islands of the Union Territory. Appropriate fare rationalisation measures may be 
explored so that air travel remains affordable for residents and tourists.  
 
TOURISM 
 
5.2.9 Tourism development plays a crucial role in t he economic, social and cultural growth of 
the Union territory of Andaman and Nicobar Islands.  Due to its natural beauty, biodiversity and 
strategic location, tourism is one of the most impo rtant sectors for the islands’ development. It 
has vast potential of generating revenue for the is lands. It creates direct employment in hotels, 
tour operators and transport services. At the same time, it creates avenues for fisheries, 
handicrafts, local markets and can encourage entrepreneurship among local population. 
 
5.2.10 The Committee noted with interest the sharp rise in tourist arrivals in the Andaman and 
Nicobar Islands, increasing from 7.22 lakh in 2024 to 8.23 lakh in 2025. The Administration 
informed that five major resort projects under the Public-Private Partnership (PPP) mode have 
been successfully bid out, drawing strong market re sponse from reputed international hospitality 
groups. The Committee further noted the proposal to  open eleven uninhabited islands for 

79 
 
regulated day-tourism in coordination with the Fore st Department. While welcoming these 
initiatives aimed at enhancing tourism infrastructure and revenue, the Committee emphasized the 
importance of maintaining world-class standards consistent with global eco-tourism benchmarks.  
 
Observation / Recommendation 
 
5.2.11 The Committee recommends that PPP tourism pr ojects adhere to internationally 
recognised environmental and construction norms. Co mprehensive environmental impact 
assessments and carrying-capacity studies may be co nducted prior to project 
implementation. Provisions for local employment gen eration and skill development may be 
incorporated in the agreements so that the local po pulation benefits from tourism 
development. A robust monitoring mechanism may be i nstituted to ensure that ecological 
sustainability is not compromised in the expansion of tourism activities. 
 
5.2.12 The Committee recommends that the impact of development in tourism sector on 
revenue generation in the UT and on the socio econo mic growth of the local population of 
the A&N Islands may be assessed. The benefits of ec onomic growth in the UT must also 
percolate to the local population as they equally d eserve to reap those benefits. The 
Committee recommends that the UT Administration exp lore opportunities for developing 
tourism-related activities in islands that are pres ently uninhabited but have scenic beaches 
and potential for eco-tourism, and develop them as tourist destinations for the growing 
number of visitors to the UT. 
 
DRINKING WATER SUPPLY AND SANITATION 
 
5.2.13 The Union territory Andaman and Nicobar Isla nds consists of many scattered islands 
with limited fresh water sources. The information w as sought about the exploring the 
proposals for the rainwater harvesting systems to a ddress the drinking water shortage in 
different habitable islands of the UT. It was infor med that drinking water is provided 
through household tap connections and community/pub lic outlets. For Rain water 
harvesting, UT has already initiated 10 projects fo r improving water storage capacity in 
various islands. In addition, the Rutland phase-II project in South Andamans is under 
advanced stage of execution. 
5.2.14 The allocation for the UT’s Scheme on Water Supply and Sanitation has increased to 
₹163.50 crores in BE 2026-27 from ₹123.59 crores in  RE 2025-26. Major projects include 
retrofitting and rehabilitation of rural pipeline n etworks; Karuppaswamy, Bachra Pahar, 
Koila Nallah, Korang Nallah and Khudirampur Nallah water supply schemes; and 
installation of 5 MLD rapid sand filters at Jawahar  Sarovar and Birdline. Ten rainwater 
harvesting projects have been initiated and the Rut land Phase-II project in South Andaman 
is at an advanced stage. 
 
 

80 
 
Observation / Recommendation 
 
5.2.15 The Committee recommends to adhere to the ti melines for pipeline 
rehabilitation, filtration infrastructure and stora ge augmentation works. Periodic 
assessment of supply reliability and water quality parameters may be undertaken to 
ensure long-term water security, particularly in remote and water-stressed islands. 
 
DISASTER PREPAREDNESS 
 
5.2.16 The Committee notes that the geographical lo cation of the Andaman and Nicobar 
Islands makes the UT highly vulnerable to cyclones,  earthquakes and tsunamis. The India 
Meteorological Department (IMD) functions as the no dal agency for cyclone warnings. The 
Committee was informed that the UT Administration h as established Emergency Operation 
Centres at the UT, district and sub-divisional leve ls and commissioned 35 manually 
operable and 2 automated tsunami sirens. Early warn ings are disseminated through 
television, radio, social media, sirens, megaphones  and mobile-based systems such as SMS 
Burst and CAP SACHET. A State-level mock exercise c onducted on 04.11.2025 covered all 
inhabited islands with participation of about 59,60 0 persons, in addition to multiple 
community-level drills and training programmes during the year. 
 
5.2.17 The capital outlay for Disaster Management h as increased from ₹3.84 crores in RE 
2025-26 to ₹1.66crores in BE 2026-27. The UT has pr oposed installation of 35 additional 
automated tsunami sirens at VSAT-based locations co -located with GPS observatories of 
INCOIS, with centralised activation at the Director ate of Disaster Management, Sri Vijaya 
Puram. 
 
Observation/ Recommendation 
 
5.2.18 The Committee observes that timely dissemina tion of early warnings and 
community preparedness of disaster management are c ritical in an island territory 
where evacuation windows are limited. The Committee  recommends expansion of 
automated siren coverage to all vulnerable coastal locations, periodic audit of 
dissemination response time across communication ch annels and establishment of 
maintenance protocols for warning infrastructure. R egular mock drills and capacity-
building programmes may continue to ensure sustained preparedness. 
 
FORESTRY, WILDLIFE AND BIODIVERSITY PROTECTION 
 
5.2.19 The allocation under Forestry and Wildlife h as increased to ₹38.91 crores in BE 2026-27 
from ₹31.92 crores in RE 2025-26. The Committee sou ght details of the conservation, 
afforestation and wildlife protection measures prop osed, particularly in ecologically sensitive 
and tribal areas of the Union Territory. 

81 
 
 
The Ministry has informed that the allocation is based on the following key proposals: 
 Phase-wise modernization of Government Saw Mill Chatham. 
 Phase-wise Survey of all National parks and sanctua ries and preparation of management 
plan. 
 Phase-wise procurement of high-speed boats and vehi cles for Joint Patrolling Team’s for 
mitigation of human crocodile conflict. 
 
5.2.20 Additionally, the process of digitization of  forest boundaries in the Islands is underway. 
The Forest Department is also undertaking regular m andated activities for protection and 
conservation of forests and other ecologically sens itive ecosystems across both tribal and non-
tribal areas. 
 
5.2.21 The Committee also sought information on mea sures taken to protect natural habitats and 
endangered species’ nesting grounds, particularly t he leatherback sea turtle and on inter-agency 
coordination mechanisms. 
 
5.2.22 The Ministry has informed that: 
 Turtle nesting beaches have been declared as Protected Areas. 
 Hatcheries are maintained to safeguard eggs and hatchlings are released into the sea. 
 The UT Administration is collaborating with the Wil dlife Institute of India for 
conservation of sea turtles. 
 The Leatherback Wildlife Sanctuary at Little Nicoba r has been notified specifically 
for protection and conservation of Leatherback Turtles. 
 
 
5.2.23 The Ministry has stated that the above measu res, along with preparation of management 
plans for National Parks and Sanctuaries and digiti zation of forest boundaries, will strengthen 
monitoring and regulatory enforcement in forest and wildlife areas. 
 
Observation/ Recommendation 
 
5.2.24 The Committee notes that the Islands possess  ecologically sensitive coastal and 
marine ecosystems, including coral reefs, mangroves  and nesting grounds of endangered 
species. Given increasing tourism, infrastructure e xpansion and climate-related 
vulnerabilities, sustained conservation efforts assume critical importance.  
5.2.25 The Committee recommends that habitat protec tion measures be strengthened 
through periodic scientific population assessments,  monitoring of nesting sites and strict 
enforcement against encroachments and unregulated a ctivities. Inter-agency coordination 
among the UT Administration, the Ministry of Enviro nment, Forest and Climate Change 
and other concerned authorities may be institutiona lized to ensure cohesive 

82 
 
implementation. Community-based conservation initia tives and awareness programmes 
may also be expanded to ensure long-term ecological sustainability of the Islands.  
 
AGRICULTURE AND ALLIED ACTIVITIES  
 
5.2.26 The allocation for Agriculture and Allied Ac tivities has increased to ₹34.81 crores in BE 
2026-27 from ₹26.34 crores in RE 2025-26. The Commi ttee sought details of the targeted 
interventions proposed under Fisheries, Animal Husb andry and Minor Irrigation and how these 
would contribute to livelihood enhancement and food security in the Islands. 
 
The Ministry has informed that the following interventions are proposed: 
 
a) Fisheries: Strengthening of primary production syst ems, reduce post-harvest and supply 
chain losses, increase local food availability and nutritional security, enhance income 
and employment opportunities for island communities etc. 
 
b) Animal Husbandry: infrastructure strengthening, inc rease in supply of farm input, 
encourage new entrepreneurs, health coverage, skill development etc.  
 
c) Minor Irrigation: subsidy to farmers for creation o f water resources (ponds, ring wells), 
purchase of pump sets and installation of micro-irr igation systems, Grants to PRIs for 
construction of check dams. 
 
The Ministry has stated that these initiatives are aimed at enhancing livelihood 
opportunities, increasing income generation and improving food security in the Islands. 
 
5.2.27 The Committee was informed about the ‘Spice Pravah’ initiative aimed at diversifying 
agriculture in the Andaman and Nicobar Islands thro ugh cultivation of high-value spices such as 
cinnamon, nutmeg, pepper and cloves over approximat ely 1,000 hectares, with a target of 10 
lakh plantations, out of which 1.6 lakh have alread y been planted. It was further indicated that 
cinnamon cultivation alone has the potential to reduce national imports by up to 40 percent.  
 
5.2.28 The Committee was informed that the Governme nt of India has approved an Integrated 
Fishing Harbour project in the Andaman and Nicobar Islands at an estimated cost of ₹199 crores 
and that the Islands have been designated as a Tuna  Cluster under the new Exclusive Economic 
Zone (EEZ) fishing policy.  
 
Observation/ Recommendation 
 
5.2.29 The Committee appreciates the initiative of Spice Pravah and recommends 
convergence with the Ministry of Agriculture for pr ovision of technical support, 
establishment of spice processing and branding unit s in the Islands and facilitation of GI-

83 
 
tag promotion and export market linkages to maximis e value realisation and farmer 
income. 
 
5.2.30 The Committee notes that the Government of I ndia has approved an Integrated 
Fishing Harbour project in the Andaman and Nicobar Islands at an estimated cost of ₹199 
crores. The Committee also observes that the fisher ies sector in the Islands has 
considerable potential for increasing income, emplo yment and export of fish products. The 
Committee recommends that the preparation of the De tailed Project Report (DPR) and 
execution of the Integrated Fishing Harbour be comp leted in a time-bound and 
coordinated manner. Associated infrastructure such as cold-chain systems, fish processing 
and processing facilities may be developed concurre ntly to ensure end-to-end efficiency. 
Export-related infrastructure may also be strengthe ned with a view to maximising revenue 
generation and harnessing the full potential of the region’s marine resources. 
 
POWER SECTOR 
 
5.2.31 The information was sought about the status of power consumption and power supply in 
the UT of Andaman and Nicobar Islands, and the perc entage of power generation from 
renewable energy sources. The Committee was informe d that power demand in entire A&N 
Islands is 65 MW, against which available capacity is 60 MW. Except Sri Vijaya Puram, there is 
no power gap in other islands. A 5 MW power plant i s under test run which will meet the 
existing gap. UT is also in the process of augmenti ng the capacity by 10 MW by April, 2026 and 
another 12.5 MW by July, 2026. 
 
5.2.32 The percentage of power generated from renew able energy sources during FY 24-25 is 
28%. In addition to this, 20.95 MW ground mounted s olar is being implemented at 08 
locations/islands. Further, 3.45 MW rooftop solar o n Government buildings is expected to be 
commissioned by December, 2026. 
 
5.2.33 The Committee noted that power generation in  the Andaman and Nicobar Islands 
continues to rely significantly on diesel-based sou rces. It was informed that a 50 MW LNG-
based power plant has been sanctioned and that 54 M W of solar power projects are under 
tendering through the Renewable Energy Service Comp anies (RESCO) model. The Committee 
also took note of the proposal for grid connectivit y across North and South Andaman, which is 
expected to benefit 80-85% of the population.  
 
5.2.34 The Committee noted that allocation for Powe r Sector was ₹708.63 crores in BE 2025-26 
and it was enhanced to ₹853.62 crores in RE 2025–26 . However, it has declined to ₹700.29 
crores in BE 2026–27. In this regard, it was appris ed that the reason for the reduction in 
allocation was due to an additional amount of ₹144. 99 crores was allocated in RE 2025-26 over 
the BE 2025-26 to meet the requirement towards the procurement of HSD for running of Power 
Plants, transportation charges of HSD, power purcha se etc. For the upcoming financial year, 

84 
 
additional requirement, if any, shall be projected in the RE 2026-27. It was also apprised that an 
amount of ₹11.00 crores has been earmarked specifically for renewable energy projects to reduce 
dependence on diesel-based power generation 
 
Observation/ Recommendation 
 
5.2.35 The UT of Andaman and Nicobar Islands has a strong potential of multiple 
renewable energy sources due to its tropical climat e and its geography. The Committee 
recommends that renewable energy capacity addition be expedited to substantially reduce 
diesel dependence and simultaneously grid connectiv ity works be completed on priority. 
The Ministry of Home Affairs along with the UT admi nistration may approach the 
concerned Ministry/ Department to bring the new pro posals of producing electricity 
through renewable energy resources, to reduce the d ependency on non-renewable sources 
of energy. The measurable annual targets for enhanc ement of renewable energy share may 
be fixed and monitored regularly. 
 
ROAD CONNECTIVITY 
 
5.2.36 The Committee noted that under the current g uidelines of the Pradhan Mantri Gram 
Sadak Yojana (PMGSY), road connectivity under PMGSY  is generally provided to habitations 
with a minimum population of 250 persons. However, The UT administration brought to the 
Committee’s attention during meeting that approxima tely 60 habitations in the Andaman and 
Nicobar Islands fall below this threshold, with pop ulation clusters ranging between 100 to 150 
persons. These habitations, many of which are located in tribal and geographically isolated areas, 
continue to face acute connectivity challenges. The  Committee also observed that such a 
constraint is not unique to the Andaman and Nicobar  Islands, but is also relevant to other Union 
Territories with sparsely populated settlements such as Ladakh. 
 
Observation/ Recommendation 
 
5.2.37 The Committee recommends that the Ministry o f Home Affairs may pursue this 
matter with the Ministry of Rural Development that it may examine the feasibility of 
relaxing the existing population norms under PMGSY for islands’ territories and other 
similarly placed regions. A dedicated flexi-provisi on may be incorporated in the scheme to 
allow inclusion of habitations that are critically in need of road connectivity despite having 
population below the threshold.

has informed that 358 buses are currently operation al in Chandigarh, including 80 electric buses. 
An additional 100 electric buses are under procurem ent and 328 more have been approved under 
the PM e-Bus Sewa scheme, of which 25 have already been received. 
5.3.14 To support electrification of public transpo rt, 56 EV charging stations and 31 Battery 
Swapping Stations have been established. The city a lso has approximately 200 kilometres of 
dedicated cycle tracks, reflecting its planned urban design. 
5.3.15 The Committee further notes that measures su ch as implementation of Adaptive Traffic 
Control Systems, establishment of an Integrated Command and Control Centre, deployment of 
Vehicle Actuated Control Systems, manual deployment of traffic personnel at congestion-prone 
corridors, strict enforcement of traffic rules and joint stakeholder surveys at bottleneck locations 
have been undertaken to reduce congestion and vehicular pollution. 
Observation/ Recommendation 
5.3.16 The Committee appreciates the emphasis laid on electric mobility and traffic 
management systems, which are critical in maintaini ng Chandigarh’s urban sustainability 
profile. Further, the Committee recommends that the  UT Administration may accelerate 
the phased induction of electric buses and ensure o ptimal utilisation of charging 
infrastructure and periodic assessment of congestio n levels and vehicular emission trends, 
with measurable indicators to evaluate measures or initiatives taken to improve 
transportation and traffic movement.

addition, digital coverage and student performance metrics at RE stage. 
 
5.3.22 During the meeting of the Committee, it obse rved that in Chandigarh, given its urban 
character, increasing longevity and prevalence of n uclear families, the number of elderly persons 
living alone may also be significant. The Committee , therefore, enquired from the UT 
administration whether it has done any exercise to compile data in respect of number of 
households where senior citizens aged sixty years a nd above are residing alone. The Committee 
is of the view that absence of reliable and disaggr egated data may constrain effective planning in 
areas such as healthcare outreach, emergency respon se, social security and safety mechanisms 
for the elderly. 
Observation/ Recommendation 
5.3.23 The Committee considers it necessary that de mographic mapping of elderly persons 
living alone be undertaken to enable targeted inter ventions and coordinated service 
delivery across departments. Therefore, the Committ ee recommends that the UT 
Administration of Chandigarh may conduct a structur ed survey to identify households 
where persons aged 60 years and above are living al one, with categorisation based on age 
group, gender and vulnerability. The findings may b e used to design an integrated support 
framework including periodic welfare checks, streng thened emergency response linkages 
and improved access to social welfare schemes.
