HEALTH 
 
5.5.4 The Committee sought detailed information reg arding the steps being taken to improve 
healthcare access in remote and difficult areas of the Union Territory of Ladakh, particularly with 
regard to strengthening of infrastructure, availabi lity of medical personnel and emergency 
response mechanisms. 
 
5.5.5 The Ministry, in its reply, informed that sig nificant measures have been undertaken to 
enhance healthcare access across the geographically challenging terrain of Ladakh.  
1.
 Health Care Infrastructure 
 Ladakh has District Hospitals at the district level , Community Health Centres (CHCs) 
and Primary Health Centres (PHCs) at the block leve l and Ayushman Arogya Mandir-
Sub Health Centres (AAM-SHCs) at the village level,  with a total of 335 health 
facilities. 
 All 32 Primary Health Centres (PHCs) and 288 Sub-Health Centres (SHCs) in the Union 
Territory have been upgraded to Ayushman Arogya Man dirs (AAMs) under the 
Ayushman Bharat initiative.  
 These facilities deliver services across 12 identif ied service domains. District Hospitals 
function as referral units and cater to all medical emergencies.  
 Medical camps are conducted regularly in the remote st and hard-to-reach areas. 
Essential drugs and a defined package of diagnostic  tests are provided free of cost at all 
levels to ensure timely diagnosis and treatment. 
 
2.
 Medical Staff Availability 
 Ayushman Arogya Mandirs are staffed with essential human resources, including 288 
Community Health Officers (CHOs) at SHCs and one Me dical Officer at each PHC, 
supported by 651 ASHAs and 555 ANMs, to deliver com prehensive clinical and public 
health services. 
 In addition, the following medical personnel are in position across the Union Territory: 
o 98 Specialist Consultants and 01 Super-Specialist 
o 169 Medical Officers 
o 35 Dental Surgeons 
o 45 AYUSH Doctors 
3. Emergency Services 

96 
 
 A structured referral and follow-up mechanism is in  place between SHCs, PHCs, CHCs 
and District Hospitals to ensure timely referrals, especially for emergencies and chronic 
conditions in remote and hard-to-reach areas of Ladakh. 
 To enhance healthcare accessibility in high-risk zo nes, 27 ambulance stations with 108 
ambulance services are strategically positioned acr oss Ladakh to enable rapid 
emergency response. Tele-medicine services are also  operational, extending healthcare 
services up to the village level. 
 
5.5.6 The Committee also sought information regardi ng the effective implementation of 
Ayushman Bharat-PMJAY in Ladakh and the challenges faced therein. The Ministry informed 
that Universal Health Coverage (UHC) was launched i n convergence with PM-JAY in December 
2020 to ensure coverage of remaining families. At p resent, 12,120 families are covered under 
AB-PMJAY, fully sponsored by the Government of Indi a. Outreach initiatives such as 
“AapkeDwar Ayushman” and Health Mela campaigns have  been conducted in remote areas 
including Nubra, Zanskar, Nyoma, Hanle, Chumathang,  Drass and Batalik to enhance awareness 
and facilitate enrolment. Internal block-wise and t ehsil-wise targets have been fixed for issuance 
of Ayushman Golden Cards to enable access to cashle ss treatment. In view of the absence of a 
tertiary care centre in Ladakh, the UT Administrati on provides travel expense support to 
beneficiaries referred outside the UT for advanced treatment. 
 
5.5.7 Further, with regard to budgetary allocation,  the Committee noted that ₹68.44 crores has 
been provided under Medical and Public Health in BE  2026-27. The Ministry informed that 
₹67.19 crores of this allocation pertains to the re venue component, primarily for heating charges 
of District Hospitals, PHCs, CHCs and GNM School an d for procurement of medical supplies 
and essential drugs. An amount of ₹1.25 crores unde r the capital component is earmarked for 
completion of ongoing works at SNM Hospital, Leh an d Sub-District Hospital, Drass. The 
Ministry further stated that additional funds, if r equired, would be projected at the Revised 
Estimates stage. 
 
Observation/ Recommendation 
 
5.5.8 The Committee appreciates the efforts made to  strengthen healthcare delivery in 
the remote and high-altitude regions of Ladakh, inc luding upgradation of PHCs and Sub-
Health Centres into Ayushman Arogya Mandirs, deploy ment of medical personnel, 
operationalisation of tele-medicine services and es tablishment of structured referral 
mechanisms. However, the Committee observes that La dakh continues to face unique 
geographical and climatic challenges which directly  impact healthcare delivery. The 
absence of a tertiary care centre within the Union Territory compels patients to travel 
outside the UT for advanced treatment. While travel  assistance is being provided, such 
referrals may cause delay in critical care, particu larly during winter months when 
connectivity is severely affected. 
 

97 
 
5.5.9 The Committee further notes that a substantia l portion of the allocation under 
Medical and Public Health for 2026-27 is towards re venue expenditure, including heating 
charges and procurement of essential supplies, whil e the capital component remains 
limited. Considering the strategic and sensitive lo cation of the UT and its dispersed 
population, strengthening of permanent health infrastructure assumes special importance. 
 
5.5.10 The Committee recommends that the feasibilit y of establishing tertiary-level 
healthcare facilities in Ladakh may be examined on priority so as to reduce dependence on 
referrals outside the UT. Availability of specialis t and super-specialist may be strengthened 
through incentive-based postings, rotational deputa tions or tele-specialist support. Capital 
investment in critical health infrastructure, parti cularly in District Hospitals and 
emergency care facilities, may be enhanced in a pha sed manner. Emergency response 
systems may be further strengthened keeping in view  extreme weather conditions and 
accessibility constraints. Implementation of Ayushm an Bharat – Pradhan Mantri Jan 
Arogya Yojana (PM-JAY) and Universal Health Coverag e (UHC) may be periodically 
reviewed to ensure universal coverage and timely se ttlement of claims.  Sustained and 
focused attention to healthcare in Ladakh remains e ssential in view of its terrain, climatic 
conditions and strategic importance. 
 
TOURISM 
 
5.5.11 The Committee noted that the allocation unde r Tourism has marginally increased to 
₹14.36 crores in BE 2026-27 from ₹12.09 crores in R E 2025-26. The Committee sought details 
of the initiatives proposed for sustainable tourism  development and capacity building, 
particularly in ecologically sensitive zones. 
 
5.5.12 The Ministry, in its reply, informed that to urism in Ladakh is being developed around 
four strategic pillars, namely Adventure Tourism, N ature-based Tourism (including Dark Sky 
Reserves and ecologically sensitive landscapes), Cu ltural Tourism (including the Buddhist 
Circuit and heritage sites) and Experiential Touris m. Project proposals are being prepared for 
central funding based on these pillars to strengthe n infrastructure, enhance visitor experience and 
ensure sustainable tourism growth in high-altitude and remote areas. 
 
5.5.13 The Ministry further informed that locally, the UT Administration promotes ecologically 
sustainable tourism through policy incentives such as the STP Incentive Scheme 2025 and the 
Ladakh Homestay Policy, complemented by initiatives  such as the PM Surya Ghar Muft Bijli 
Yojana. Capacity-building programmes are being cond ucted to train local youth, tourist guides, 
homestay operators and hospitality workers. Astro-t ourism initiatives centred around the Dark 
Sky Reserve at Hanle are also being promoted to pre serve night skies, promote scientific 
awareness and diversify rural incomes. 
 
5.5.14 To safeguard fragile ecosystems, the Adminis tration has proposed tourism carrying-

98 
 
capacity studies for key destinations including Pan gong Tso, Hemis National Park, Changthang 
and Zanskar. These studies are intended to provide data-driven guidance on visitor limits, 
infrastructure requirements and environmental safeg uards to ensure that tourism growth does not 
compromise ecologically sensitive landscapes. 
 
5.5.15 The Committee also sought information regard ing concrete steps taken to develop 
cultural and adventure tourism while ensuring envir onmental safeguards and community benefit 
sharing. The Ministry informed that the Registration and Renewal Guidelines for Adventure Tour 
Operators (ATOs), 2024 have been notified to regula te adventure tourism activities. Operational 
standards have been prescribed to ensure environmen tal protection, tourist safety and adherence 
to best practices. The Ladakh Mountaineering Policy , 2024 has also been introduced to promote 
safe and environmentally responsible mountaineering. 
 
5.5.16 The Ministry further stated that the Departm ent actively organises and supports 
adventure-based events and festivals such as the Ch adar Trek on the frozen Zanskar River, Ice 
Climbing Festival, Suru Summer Festival (2025 marke d its inaugural edition following Suru’s 
inclusion in National Geographic’s “25 Best Destina tions” for 2024,Ladakh Aero Festival, Pre-
Paragliding Accuracy World Cup and the Ladakh Marat hon internationally recognised as the 
“World’s Highest Marathon.”.  
 
5.5.17 In the area of cultural tourism, the Departm ent has undertaken construction of museums 
across different parts of Ladakh and extends suppor t to museums that preserve and display 
artefacts, manuscripts and religious and cultural heritage.The Department of Tourism and Culture 
also aids monasteries for civil works, beautificati on and visitor amenities, thereby improving 
infrastructure while preserving the sanctity and cu ltural significance of these sites. Additionally, 
the Department actively supports and promotes tradi tional festivals that showcase Ladakh’s rich 
cultural heritage and attract domestic and internat ional tourists. These include, but are not limited 
to, the SpitukGustor Festival, Dosmochey Festival, Hemis Tsechu Festival, ThikseyGustor 
Festival, Sindhu Darshan and the Ladakh Nomadic Fes tival. These festivals serve as platforms 
for cultural preservation, community participation and inclusive economic benefits for local 
artisans, performers and service providers. 
 
5.5.18 In addition to supporting traditional festiv als and heritage infrastructure, the Department 
of Tourism, UT Ladakh, has strengthened cultural to urism promotion through the launch of 
‘Celebrate Ladakh’ Tourism Calendar, focusing on fe stivals as key cultural tourism products and 
positioning Ladakh as a year-round destination. Und er this initiative, the department has declared 
nine UT-Level Festivals with fixed locations and da tes for the next three years, ensuring planned 
promotion, coherent branding and enhanced national and international visibility, while all other 
local and regional festivals continue to be celebra ted in accordance with community 
traditions.Collectively, these initiatives reflect a balanced approach towards tourism development 
in Ladakh, integrating environmental sustainability , safety standards, cultural preservation and 
meaningful community benefit sharing. 

99 
 
 
Observation/Recommendation 
 
5.5.19 The Committee notes the structured approach adopted by the UT Administration 
in developing tourism around adventure, nature, cul tural and experiential pillars, along 
with policy frameworks for regulating adventure act ivities and promoting community 
participation. The Committee particularly appreciat es the focus on carrying-capacity 
studies and dark sky preservation initiatives in ec ologically fragile zones. The Committee is 
of the view that carrying-capacity assessments must  be completed in a time-bound manner 
and integrated into planning and regulatory decisio ns. The Committee is of the view that 
infrastructure expansion, waste management systems,  water usage regulation and carbon 
footprint mitigation must receive priority attention. 
 
5.5.20 The Committee recommends that sustainable to urism principles may be adopted 
through guidelines, continuous environmental monito ring and stronger community-based 
tourism models to ensure equitable benefit sharing.  It also recommends that skill 
development initiatives be scaled up to maximise lo cal employment and reduce external 
dependence. The Committee underscores that tourism growth in Ladakh may remain 
environmentally responsible, culturally respectful and community-centric. 
 
POWER 
 
5.5.21 An allocation of ₹135.18 crores has been pro vided under the Power sector for 2026-27, 
with ₹93.39 crores earmarked for power purchase obl igations. The Ministry has informed that 
with the allocation towards power sector, the following initiatives are being undertaken: 
• Refurbishment of 3X1.25 MW Iqbal Hydro Project which will support local 
generation and improve grid voltage. 
• For grid reliability & renewable energy adoption, 25 MWp AC and 12 MW AC solar 
power plants are under construction under PMDP and SDP funding. 
• Improvement and stabilization of HT & LT lines for providing reliable power supply 
to the consumers of UT Ladakh. 
• Creation of different capacities of sub stations for improvement of voltage in low 
voltage areas. 
• Extension of HT & LT lines for the newly settled households. 
• Repair and maintenance of the Power infrastructures. 
• The major portion of the funds is for the purchase of Power for which, the allocation 
is ₹ 93.39 crores for the year 2026-27. 
• For reduction in diesel dependence, the remote villages shall be connected to the Grid 
supply after completion of 220 KV Transmission line from Drass to Zanskar and 220 
KV system from Phyang (Leh) to Nobra under PMDP which is under construction. 

100 
 
5.5.22 Further, the Committee sought to know a road  map on how solar initiatives will reduce 
the UT’s dependence on diesel generators, especiall y for army and civilians during the harsh 
winter months. The Ministry informed that the Depen dence on Diesel Generators will reduce 
with the installation of Off grid Solar Plants with  battery back-up which are under operation in 
small habitations of Changthang and Zanskar. The to tal dependence on DG sets shall be reduced 
only after the completion of PMDP and RDSS schemes of UT Ladakh which will bring 
Changthang, Zanskar and Nubra within the ambit of t he Grid. This will impact both Army and 
Civil habitations. This will also enable import of Power as well as export of locally generated 
solar power. 
5.5.23 Furthermore, order to promote Renewable Ener gy, the following Solar projects are being 
implemented in Ladakh: 
• SECI under PMDP is setting up a 25 MW (AC) Solar Power Project with 40 MWH BESS 
at Taru. 
• LPDD is setting up a 12 MW (AC) Solar Power Proje ct at Phey Leh under SDP budget. 
• 7 MW Solar project with 1.5 MWH BESS on RESCO mod el is being developed at Stakna 
Leh.  
• 7 MW Solar project with 1.5 MWH BESS on RESCO mod el is being developed at Tungri 
Zanskar. 
• The UT has installed 1496 Nos Rooftop Solar Plant s on Govt/School buildings with a 
cumulative capacity of 6.84 MW. 
• The UT is implementing the PM Surya Ghar Muft Bij li Scheme and has installed 1364 
Nos. Rooftop Solar till now; UT is also providing additional UT subsidy. 
• The UT is implementing the PM-KUSUM scheme wherei n 265 Nos Solar Water Lifting 
Pumps have been installed. The UT is providing addi tional subsidy up to 30% of the 
MNRE approved benchmark cost. 
Observation /Recommendation 
 
5.5.24 The Committee notes that the Union Territory  of Ladakh possesses considerable 
potential for generation of renewable energy, parti cularly solar energy, owing to its high 
altitude, clear skies and favourable climatic condi tions. The Committee appreciates that 
the UT Administration has initiated measures to harness renewable energy resources in the 
region. However, considering the growing energy req uirements of the Union Territory and 
its dependence on external sources of power, optima l utilisation of locally available 
renewable energy assumes greater importance. 
5.5.25 The Committee recommends that the UT Adminis tration may adopt a 
comprehensive strategy for development of solar and  wind energy in the region so as to 

101 
 
utilise the available renewable energy potential in  a planned and time-bound manner. The 
share of power generated from solar energy may be p rogressively enhanced and concrete 
steps may be taken to assess and develop the wind e nergy potential of the Union Territory. 
Periodic monitoring of renewable energy projects ma y also be undertaken to ensure timely 
implementation and effective utilisation of resources. 
 
WATER SUPPLY, SANITATION AND SOLID WASTE MANAGEMENT 
 
5.5.26 The Committee noted that the allocation for Water Supply and Sanitation has declined 
from ₹11.30 crores in RE 2025-26 to ₹9.80 crores in  BE 2026-27. The Committee sought to 
know how the UT Administration proposes to address drinking water scarcity and sanitation 
challenges in remote and high-altitude habitations in view of the reduced allocation. 
 
5.5.27 The Ministry, in its reply, informed that th e reduction under this head is primarily on 
account of Fuels and Lubricants. The enhanced provi sions in RE 2025-26 were made to clear 
pending liabilities of the previous year. The alloc ations in BE 2026-27 have been rationalized in 
order to adhere to the revenue and capital expendit ure ceilings prescribed by the Ministry of 
Finance. 
 
5.5.28 It was further informed that the allocation under this head is largely utilized for revenue 
expenditure towards fuels and lubricants for water tankers, procurement of water-supply-related 
spare parts and stores, repair and maintenance of w ater supply schemes and tankers and payment 
of electricity dues for water lifting points. 
 
5.5.29 To address drinking water supply and sanitat ion requirements in remote and high-altitude 
habitations, the Ministry stated that any additiona l requirements will be met, as far as feasible, 
from the Tribal Area Component. Further, initiative s under Central Sponsored Schemes such as 
Jal Jeevan Mission (JJM) and AMRUT are being implem ented. Accordingly, drinking water 
supply and sanitation requirements will be addresse d through coordinated funding and 
implementation by the UT and CSS schemes in synergy. 
 
5.5.30 With regard to sustainable water management,  the Ministry informed that the 
Administration of the Union Territory of Ladakh is addressing water supply challenges and 
ensuring sustainable water resource management thro ugh a comprehensive, climate-resilient and 
institutionally coordinated approach, keeping in vi ew the region’s low rainfall, cold desert 
ecology and increasing impacts of climate change. 
 
 Under the Jal Jeevan Mission, the UT Administration , through the District Water & 
Sanitation Missions (DWSMs), is implementing measur es for planning, execution, 
monitoring and long-term sustainability of drinking water schemes.  
 The DWSMs play a pivotal role in district-level con vergence, source sustainability 
planning, monitoring of scheme performance and stre ngthening community institutions, 

102 
 
particularly Village Water & Sanitation Committees (VWSCs). 
 Sustainable water supply is being ensured through i dentification, protection and 
augmentation of reliable sources such as springs, g laciers, snow-fed streams and 
groundwater, supported by spring-shed management, c atchment area treatment and 
recharge interventions.  
 In view of climate variability and declining snowfa ll, the Administration, under the 
guidance of DWSMs, is promoting storage-based solut ions, including construction of 
reservoirs, winter water harvesting systems and ado ption of locally appropriate 
innovations such as artificial glaciers and ice stupas, which help store excess winter water 
for summer use. 
 The DWSMs are also responsible for regular water qu ality monitoring and surveillance, 
ensuring compliance with national drinking water qu ality standards through laboratory 
testing, field-level inspections and community-base d water quality monitoring. Capacity 
building of local manpower for operation and mainte nance, promotion of water 
conservation practices and community ownership of assets are being actively pursued. 
 Further, the Administration is ensuring inter-secto ral convergence at the district level 
through DWSMs with schemes related to watershed dev elopment, afforestation, rural 
livelihoods and climate adaptation, thereby strengt hening the resilience of drinking water 
systems in Ladakh’s fragile Himalayan ecosystem. 
 
5.5.31 Through these coordinated institutional and technical interventions, the Administration 
aims to ensure safe, adequate and sustainable drink ing water supply, while enhancing long-term 
resilience against climate change impacts in the Union Territory of Ladakh. 
 
SOLID WASTE MANAGEMENT 
 
5.5.32 The Committee also sought information regard ing the status of solid waste management 
and landfill infrastructure in Ladakh and the centr al support sought/received in the last 12 
months. The Ministry informed that solid waste mana gement infrastructure has been developed 
in both towns of Leh and Kargil districts for scien tific processing and disposal of municipal solid 
waste. 
 
5.5.33 In Leh District, an Integrated Solid Waste M anagement (ISWM) facility of 30 TPD 
capacity is operational at Skampari, comprising 20 TPD for dry waste and 10 TPD for wet waste 
processing. A Scientific Landfill Facility (SLF) ha s also been constructed at the site with a 
design life of 20 years. In addition, a Material Re covery Facility (MRF) named Tsangda has 
been set up at Solar Colony with a capacity of 1 TP D. A decentralized smart wet waste 
composting facility has been established at Bomgarh with a capacity of 500 kg/day. 
 
5.5.34 In Kargil District, an ISWM facility of 25 T PD capacity is operational, comprising 15 
TPD for dry waste and 10 TPD for wet waste processi ng. For scientific disposal of residual 

103 
 
waste, land has been allotted for a Sanitary Landfill Facility (SLF). The project is presently at the 
Detailed Project Report (DPR) stage and is proposed with a design capacity of 20 years. 
 
5.5.35 The Ministry further informed that central f inancial assistance has been received under 
Swachh Bharat Mission-Urban (SBM-U) 2.0 for City Sa nitation Action Plan (Toilets), IEC 
activities and Capacity Building of Urban Local Bodies. The details are as under:

Observation/Recommendation 
 
5.5.37 The Committee notes that the reduction in al location under Water Supply and 
Sanitation in BE 2026-27 is primarily due to rationalisation of expenditure and clearance of 
pending liabilities in the previous year and that t he major portion of the allocation is 
towards revenue expenditure such as fuels and lubricants, repair and maintenance of water 
supply schemes, electricity dues for water lifting points and procurement of essential stores. 
Drinking water supply and sanitation requirements in remote and high-altitude habitations 
are proposed to be addressed through the Tribal Are a Component and through 
convergence with Central Sponsored Schemes such as Jal Jeevan Mission (JJM) and 
AMRUT. Climate-resilient measures being undertaken under Jal Jeevan Mission through 
District Water & Sanitation Missions (DWSMs), inclu ding source sustainability measures, 
protection of springs and snow-fed streams, storage -based solutions such as reservoirs, 

104 
 
winter water harvesting systems, artificial glaciers and ice stupas, water quality monitoring 
and community participation. 
 
5.5.38 With regard to Solid Waste Management, the C ommittee notes that Integrated Solid 
Waste Management (ISWM) facilities are operational in Leh and Kargil, that a Scientific 
Landfill Facility has been constructed in Leh and t hat land has been allotted for a Sanitary 
Landfill Facility in Kargil. Proposals have been su bmitted under SBM-Urban 2.0 for 
strengthening Solid Waste Management infrastructure  in Leh and for establishment of a 
Sewage Treatment Plant in Kargil. 
 
5.5.39 The Committee observes that Ladakh’s fragile  ecosystem, high-altitude terrain, low 
rainfall and increasing tourism and urbanisation pr essures require sustained and forward-
looking investment in drinking water supply, sanita tion and scientific management of solid 
and liquid waste.  
 
5.5.40 The Committee recommends that drinking water  security and source sustainability 
be treated as priority areas, with focused capital investment in storage, recharge and 
climate-resilient infrastructure. Proposals submitt ed under SBM-Urban 2.0 for 
strengthening Solid Waste Management and Used Water  Management be expedited for 
early approval and time-bound implementation. Decen tralised waste processing, safe 
landfill practices, operationalisation of the propo sed Sewage Treatment Plant (STP) at 
Kargil and strict monitoring of water quality and w aste disposal systems be ensured. An 
integrated and climate-resilient approach combining  water supply, sanitation and waste 
management be adopted to safeguard Ladakh’s fragile  Himalayan environment while 
meeting the needs of remote and tribal habitations. 
 
CIVIL AVIATION 
5.5.41 An amount of ₹22.13 crores has been allocate d under Civil Aviation in BE 2026-27. The 
Ministry has informed about the following initiatives taken in this regard: 
Ongoing Helicopter Service: 
• The Department of Civil Aviation, UT of Ladakh, is presently operating Single 
Engine B3 helicopter services with routes originati ng from Leh to Kargil, Zanskar, 
Srinagar Lingshied, Dibling, Neerak, Diskit (Nubra) and Turtuk (8 locations).  
 
• Additionally, within Leh District, helicopter opera tions can be undertaken on a 
need/demand basis to Lukung, Hanley, Shashukul, Pho toksar, Shyok and 
LargyabYokma, Sumur (07 locations).  
 
• In Kargil District, helicopter operations can be co nducted to Drass, Wakha, 
Heniskote, Sapi, Silmo, Chintan, Chechesena, Tangole, Rangdum, Barsoo and Zangla 
(11 locations) on a need/demand basis, particularly  during disaster management 

105 
 
situations and medical/patient evacuation emergencies. 
 
Proposed Air Connectivity Initiatives: 
5.5.42 Exploration of the possibility of Fixed Wing  (9-11-seater) / Double Engine Helicopter 
operations under UDAN Scheme on the following route s: (i) Kargil - Jammu - Kargil (ii) Kargil 
- Srinagar - Kargil (iii) Thoise- Chandigarh -Thoise. 
 
Role in Improving Accessibility to Remote Regions: 
5.5.43 The service ensures critical last-mile conne ctivity to far-flung, high-altitude and border 
areas where surface transport is constrained due to  terrain and weather. It plays a vital role in 
emergency medical evacuations, disaster response, a dministrative outreach and connectivity to 
remote habitations, thereby significantly reducing response time and improving access to 
essential services. 
5.5.44 The Committee notes that the Ministry has in formed that under the Tribal Area 
Component, 25 works relating to Civil Aviation have  been taken up with an estimated cost of 
₹35.74 crores, reflecting the continued emphasis on  strengthening air connectivity in the Union 
Territory of Ladakh. The Ministry has further indic ated that these interventions are aimed at 
augmenting aviation-related infrastructure to addre ss geographic isolation, seasonal 
inaccessibility and rising passenger movement linke d to tourism, defence requirements and 
medical travel. 
 
Observation/ Recommendation 
 
5.5.45 The Committee, while appreciating the effort s made, observes that in a high-altitude 
and strategically sensitive region such as Ladakh, civil aviation functions as a critical 
lifeline service rather than merely a mode of trans port. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that priority be accorded to strengtheni ng airport infrastructure at Leh and 
Kargil, including augmentation of terminal and apro n capacity to manage peak tourist and 
emergency traffic efficiently. Sustained and reliab le air connectivity to Kargil and other 
remote sectors may be ensured through appropriate p olicy support. Dedicated air cargo 
facilities may also be developed to facilitate tran sportation of horticulture and other 
perishable produce, thereby supporting local liveli hoods. In addition, necessary measures 
may be taken to enhance winter-operational capabili ty and ensure dependable all-weather 
aviation services in view of the extreme climatic conditions of the region.

progress and ensure transparency and administrative  efficiency. Timely recruitment will 
strengthen governance capacity and provide meaningf ul employment opportunities to 
educated youth of Ladakh within the Union Territory.

TRANSPORT AND CONNECTIVITY 
 
5.6.5 Transport and connectivity constitute a major  component of the Budget of the Union 
Territory of Lakshadweep, considering its dispersed island geography and dependence on sea and 
air links for movement of passengers, essential commodities and emergency services. 
 
5.6.6 Allocation under Transport is ₹547.39 crores in BE 2026-27. The UT Administration 
proposes to utilise this allocation for improving s hip-to-shore connectivity, inter-island transport 
and air connectivity and the funds will be utilized for: 
 
1.
 Acquisition Operation, maintenance and repair of ships.  
2. Construction and maintenance of jetties and berths.  
3. Acquisition of boats, barges and ship-to-shore facilities.  
4. Ship-to-shore transportation charges. 
5. Port workshop operations and maintenance of equipment.  
6. Operation and maintenance of helicopters mainly for  air ambulance and emergency 
services. 
7. Development and expansion of helipads and airport infrastructure. 
 
5.6.7 The Ministry has further informed that capita l expenditure under Transport has increased 
to ₹39.87 crores in BE 2026-27 to strengthen inter-island and mainland connectivity. 
 
1. With regard to Shipping Sector, the Ministry has informed that: 
Shipping Sector: Inter Island / Mainland - Island / Port / Jetty / Ferry 
 Port Infrastructure Projects: UT has submitted 15 p roposals for  Jetties and Break 
water to MoPSW, for consideration under Sagarmala Scheme.  
 
 Ship Acquisitions:UT has submitted various proposal s for acquisition of All 
Weather Passenger Ships / Cargo Barges and Replacem ent of High Speed Crafts (Fair 
Weather) with All-Weather High-Speed Crafts to MoPS W for consideration under 
Sagarmala Scheme. 
 
2. With regard to air connectivity, the Ministry has informed that: 
Air Connectivity: Airport Infrastructure / Flights 

110 
 
 Agatti Airport Expansion: The UT has taken up the p roposal to expand the Agatti 
Airport (Runway length of 2480 m) to make it suitab le for A - 320 Type Aircrafts. 
Further, this Airport would be a dual use Airport w hich would also serve the Defense 
requirements. 
 Greenfield Airport in Minicoy: UT also proposes to establish a dual use Airport in 
Minicoy to meet the requirements of Civil and Defense establishments.  
5.6.8 The Committee expressed concern regarding hig h airfares on commercial flights to 
Agatti and the desirability of support under the UD AN scheme. Concerns were also raised 
regarding the operational reliability of seaplane a nd small-aircraft services. The UT 
Administration informed that flight frequency has b een enhanced with the addition of Fly91 
services and that seaplane operations are proposed to be rolled out at Kalpeni, Agatti and other 
islands. 
Observation/ Recommendation 
5.6.9 The Committee notes that transport connectivi ty in Lakshadweep is a lifeline 
service and that affordability, reliability and con tinuity of essential services must be 
ensured alongside infrastructure expansion. The Com mittee recommends that the 
Administration may explore support mechanisms such as UDAN, viability gap funding or 
seat subvention for commercially priced yet sociall y essential routes such as Agatti-Kochi. 
The proposals submitted under Sagarmala may be fina lised expeditiously and acquisition 
of all-weather vessels may be prioritised to reduce weather-related isolation.  
URBAN DEVELOPMENT, HOUSING, WATER SUPPLY AND FLOOD CONTROL  
5.6.10 Urban development and basic civic infrastruc ture remain critical sectors for the Union 
Territory of Lakshadweep in view of its limited lan d availability, fragile coastal ecosystem and 
vulnerability to climate-related risks. Allocation under Urban Development, Housing, Water 
Supply and Flood Control has been ₹61.28 crores in BE 2026-27.  
5.6.11 With regard to water security, the Ministry has informed that the UT continues to rely 
significantly on desalination technologies, includi ng Low Temperature Thermal Desalination 
(LTTD) plants, in addition to rainwater harvesting systems. The Ministry has informed that 
ongoing projects aim at ensuring year-round availab ility of potable water across inhabited 
islands. Funds are also being utilised for maintena nce and upgradation of existing distribution 
networks to reduce transmission losses. Further, de salination plants have been installed in all 
islands, except Andrott, where the Low Temperature Thermal Desalination plant (LTTD) is 
expected to be completed by April, 2026.  
 
5.6.12 Further, in the area of coastal protection a nd flood control, scientific shoreline 
management plans have been prepared in consultation  with expert agencies. Anti-sea erosion 
measures are being addressed through scientific sho reline management plans prepared by 

111 
 
National Centre for Sustainable Coastal Management (NCSCM), following the discontinuation 
of hard coastal structures as per Ministry of Envir onment, Forest and Climate Change 
(MoEFCC) directions. Diaphragm walls along peripher al roads are currently proposed to protect 
land and infrastructure from wave action and erosion. 
 
Observation/ Recommendation 
5.6.13 The Committee observes that in small island territories, urban development and 
water supply are directly linked to public health, tourism sustainability and environmental 
stability. Any delay in desalination or drainage pr ojects can have immediate consequences 
on quality of life and economic activity. The Commi ttee notes that the success of urban 
development interventions in Lakshadweep depends on  sustainable planning, strict 
adherence to Coastal Regulation Zone norms and time ly completion of projects. Water 
security measures, particularly desalination, requi re continuous technical supervision and 
maintenance.  
5.6.14 The Committee recommends that ongoing desali nation and water augmentation 
projects be completed in a time-bound manner and th at regular monitoring of water 
quality and supply hours be managed properly. Urban  drainage and flood mitigation 
works may be prioritised in vulnerable islands to a ddress climate-related risks. Coastal 
protection projects may comply with environmental s afeguards and adopt eco-sensitive 
methods. Periodic reporting on physical and financi al progress of urban infrastructure 
projects may also be placed before the Monitoring a uthority to ensure effective utilisation 
of funds.

allocations have been made in BE 2026-27 for streng thening school infrastructure, improving 
educational facilities and promoting higher and technical education in the islands.

5.6.17 During the meeting of the Committee, Members  raised concerns regarding adequacy of 
infrastructure in certain schools, continuity of ed ucational services during reconstruction or 
relocation of facilities, availability of qualified  teaching staff and the need to ensure that student s 
are not adversely affected during transition phases . It was highlighted that in small island 
territories, disruption of even a single educationa l institution can significantly affect students due  
to lack of alternative facilities. Members also hig hlighted the need to align education and skill 
development programmes with local employment opport unities, particularly in tourism, marine 
services, renewable energy and environmental management. 
 
Observation/ Recommendation 
 
5.6.18  The Committee notes that education planning in Laks hadweep requires careful 
consideration of geographical constraints and limit ed institutional alternatives. 
Infrastructure redevelopment in such a context must  be undertaken in a phased and 
coordinated manner to ensure uninterrupted academic  functioning. The Committee 
recommends that reconstruction, demolition or reloc ation of school infrastructure may be 
undertaken only after ensuring fully functional int erim arrangements and appropriate 
public communication so that academic activities are not disrupted. 
 
5.6.19 The Committee further observes that technica l and vocational education may be 
aligned with emerging economic sectors in the islan ds so as to improve local employability. 
The Committee also takes note of the suggestion mad e during deliberations to explore 

113 
 
public-private partnership mechanisms and short-ter m deputation of faculty from 
mainland institutions to strengthen academic capaci ty where required. The Committee 
recommends that periodic review of infrastructure u tilisation and educational outcomes 
may be undertaken to assess the effectiveness of the fund allocations.

5.6.21 The UT operates helicopter services, which i s used as an air ambulance to transport 
patients to Kochi/ Mainland in case of a medical emergency. 
 
5.6.22 During the meeting, Members raised concerns regarding continuity of services in certain 
islands, particularly in cases where reconstruction  or redevelopment of hospital buildings is 
proposed. Concerns were also expressed regarding av ailability of specialist doctors, adequacy of 
diagnostic facilities and hardship faced by patient s referred to mainland hospitals, especially in 
Kochi. Members opined that in an island territory, health services function as an essential lifeline 
and must remain uninterrupted at all times. 
 
 
 
 

114 
 
Observation/Recommendation 
 
5.6.23  The Committee notes that due to limited tertiary ca re facilities within the islands, 
residents are significantly dependent on referral s ervices. Any disruption in primary or 
secondary healthcare facilities can therefore have serious consequences. The Committee 
also observes that shortage of specialist doctors a nd trained paramedical staff affects 
quality of care and increases dependency on mainland institutions. 
 
5.6.24 The Committee recommends that any reconstruc tion, relocation or redevelopment 
of hospital infrastructure be undertaken only after  ensuring fully functional interim 
healthcare arrangements and proper public communica tion. Deployment of specialist 
doctors and medical teams from mainland tertiary in stitutions on short-term rotational 
basis may be explored to bridge manpower gaps. Tele medicine facilities may be 
strengthened and adequate diagnostic equipment prov ided in island hospitals so as to 
reduce unnecessary referrals. A structured patient- support mechanism, including 
assistance for accommodation and logistics in mainl and cities such as Kochi, may also be 
explored to minimise hardships for referred patient s and their attendants. Periodic 
monitoring of health service delivery indicators ma y be undertaken to assess improvement 
in access and quality of care. 
 
POWER 
 
5.6.25 An allocation of ₹244.98 crores has been allocated for BE 2026-27 und er the sub head 
Power.  
Key measures implemented to reduce dependence on diesel-based power are as follows: 
a)
 Solar PV with Battery Energy Storage System (BESS) commissioned at Kavaratti and 
Agatti islands. 
b) Additional ground-mounted solar projects with BESS at Amini and Androth have been 
awarded. 
c) Tenders have been finalized and Power Purchase Agre ements are under process for 
Floating Solar Power Plants with BESS at Kavaratti and Agatti while DPR preparation is 
underway in five additional islands. 
d) Rooftop solar installations on Government buildings are under tendering process. 
e) Feasibility of offshore wind energy is being explored. 
f) The Renewable Energy Policy-2024 has been notified and Solar Energy Corporation of 
India Limited (SECI) has been designated as the nod al agency for Renewable Energy 
(RE) development. 
 
5.6.26 Further, installation of renewable sources o f energy, purchase of new generation and 
distribution equipments and its maintenance etc. ar e being prioritized. ₹12.50 crores is 
earmarked specifically for renewable energy, coveri ng feasibility studies and installation of 
solar-based power systems. 

115 
 
 
5.6.27 The UT has prepared a Detailed Renewable Ene rgy Plan for the UT of Lakshadweep 
from renewable energy sources, thereby reducing fos sil fuel usage to minimal levels and 
retaining diesel power plants as standby support. A s per the plan, a total Renewable Energy 
capacity of 71.69 MW, along with 244 MWh Battery En ergy Storage System (BESS) is required 
to achieve the above objective.  
 
5.6.28 The present renewable energy initiatives und ertaken in Lakshadweep are as follows: 
(i)
 Ground mounted solar plants 
a. Commissioned - 
1.4MWp Solar Plant with 1.4 MWh BESS at Kavaratti. 
0.3 MWp Solar Plant at Agatti. 
 These plants are generating approximately 2.33 Mill ion Units (MU) 
annually and are meeting around 10% of the energy d emand of these 
islands. 
b. Initiated- 
Work has been awarded and Power Purchase Agreement executed 
forestablishment of 0.952 MWp Solar Plant with 0.03 7 MWh BESS at 
Androth and Amini islands. 
 
Land measuring 56.17 acres has been identified at Androth, Amini, Kiltan and 
Chetlat. The land acquisition process has been initiated.  
 
(ii)
 Floating Solar plant 
a.
 Tender has been finalized and Power purchase tariff  discovered for installation of 
11.25 MWp Floating Solar plant with 32 MWh BESS at Kavaratti (6 MWp with 
16 MWH BESS) and Agatti(5.25 MWp with 16 MWH BESS).  Award of work is 
under process. 
b. The plants are expected to generate approximately 1 8.4 MU annually and meet 
nearly 80% of the energy demand of these two islands. 
c. Feasibility studies have been completed for floatin g solar plants in five islands 
having lagoon areas, viz ., Kadmat, Kalpeni, Kiltan, Chetlat and Minicoy. 
Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA) and Coastal R egulation Zone (CRZ) 
studies are in progress.  
(iii) Roof top Solar plant 
a.
 PM Surya Gharmuft Bijli Yojana (RTS on Private Buildings)-  
 The first RTS plant was commissioned in September 2 024 and Scheme is 
under active implementation 
 RTS on Government Buildings-The UT has conducted a feasibility survey 
of Government buildings across Lakshadweep and foun d 124 buildings 
technically feasible for installation of Rooftop So lar (RTS) systems 
aggregating 2.107 MW with 5.525 MWh BESS. 

116 
 
 
Observation/Recommendation 
5.6.29 The Committee notes that power supply in Lak shadweep is predominantly 
dependent on diesel-based power generation due to t he absence of mainland grid 
connectivity. While allocations have been provided for operation and maintenance of 
power stations and for introduction of renewable energy projects, recurring expenditure on 
fuel continues to impose a significant financial bu rden. The Committee also observes that 
logistical constraints and weather-related disrupti ons can affect timely fuel supply, thereby 
impacting reliability of power in the islands.  
5.6.30 The Committee recommends that although steps  have been initiated for solar power 
generation with battery storage systems, the scale of renewable integration requires further 
augmentation to ensure long-term sustainability and  energy security. The UT 
Administration may adopt a phased and time-bound st rategy to progressively reduce 
dependency on diesel-based generation by expanding solar power projects integrated with 
adequate battery energy storage systems. Capital in vestments may be prioritised towards 
strengthening renewable base-load capacity and modernising transmission and distribution 
infrastructure. An island-wise energy transition plan with measurable targets for reduction 
in diesel consumption and improvement in system eff iciency may also be prepared. 
Periodic monitoring of fuel expenditure vis-à-vis  renewable energy generation may be 
considered to ensure fiscal prudence, reliability o f supply and long-term sustainability of 
the power sector in the Union Territory. 
 
INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY 
 
5.6.31  Allocation under Information Technology has decreas ed from ₹120.00 crores in RE 
2025-26 to ₹66.34 crores in BE 2026-27. The Ministr y has informed that the enhanced allocation 
of ₹120.00 crores at the Revised Estimates stage in  2025-26 was primarily intended to clear 
pending liabilities pertaining to previous years as  well as to meet additional requirements during 
the current financial year. 
 
5.6.32 For BE 2026-27, an allocation of ₹66.34 cror es has been earmarked towards: Payments to 
ERNET India; OPEX charges for the KLI Submarine Cab le Project; Payments to BSNL; 
Maintenance of e-Office systems; and Other e-Govern ance initiatives. The Ministry has further 
stated that additional funds, if required, shall be sought at the Revised Estimates stage. 
 
Observation/ Recommendation 
5.6.33 The Committee notes that the reduction in BE  2026-27 appears to be on account of 
one-time liability clearance in RE 2025-26 rather t han curtailment of core digital 
infrastructure initiatives. However, given the stra tegic importance of digital connectivity in 

117 
 
an island territory, sustained funding is essential  for maintenance of submarine cable 
infrastructure, reliable broadband services and e-Governance systems. 
5.6.34 The Committee recommends that adequate provi sion be ensured for uninterrupted 
operation and maintenance of the submarine cable ne twork and digital service platforms. 
Considering Lakshadweep’s geographical isolation, d igital connectivity functions as a 
critical public utility. Performance benchmarks rel ating service reliability and last-mile 
connectivity may be periodically reviewed. Addition al funds, if required, may also be 
provided in a timely manner at the Revised Estimates stage to avoid service disruptions.

5.8.3 The Committee observes that the Union Territo ry projected a requirement of ₹50,640.02 
crores for BE 2026–27, whereas an allocation of ₹43 ,290.29 crores has been made, resulting in a 
shortfall of approximately ₹7,350 crores 
vis-à-vis  the projected demand. While the allocation 
under Central Assistance to Union Territories has i ncreased over BE 2025–26, it remains 
substantially lower than the projected requirement of ₹50,000 crores. The Committee further 
notes that Support for Capital Expenditure has rema ined constant at ₹101.77 crores and has been 
fully utilised during 2025–26 (till 31 st  January 2026). In the Committee’s view, given the 
infrastructure and developmental needs of the Union  Territory, there is scope for enhancing 
capital support to facilitate asset creation and long-term growth. 
 
5.8.4 Overall, the Committee is of the view that wh ile the allocation for 2026–27 reflects a 
moderate increase over the previous year, greater a lignment between projected requirements and 
approved allocations, improved planning of project- linked expenditure and enhanced focus on 
capital formation would contribute to more effective and outcome-oriented utilisation of funds. 
 
Observation/ Recommendation 
 
5.8.5 The Committee notes that as on 31 
st  January 2026, expenditure stood at ₹38,349.77 
crores against RE of ₹41,340.22 crores, indicating that a significant portion of funds was 

123 
 
yet to be utilised in the last quarter. The Committ ee further observes that support for 
Capital Expenditure has been fully utilised, reflec ting effective deployment of limited 
capital grants. Equity infusion for KIRU Hydroelect ric Project and allocation under 
Jhelum Tavi Flood Recovery Project (JTFRP–EAP) were  made at RE stage, and 
expenditure is contingent upon project progress. Di saster Response Fund utilisation is 
demand-driven and depends on the occurrence of disasters. 
  
5.8.6 The Committee recommends that project-linked allocations, particularly under 
hydropower and externally aided flood recovery proj ects, may be synchronised with 
realistic implementation timelines. Efforts may be made to avoid bunching of expenditure 
in the last quarter. Periodic review mechanisms may  be strengthened to ensure that 
allocations translate into tangible physical outcom es, especially in infrastructure and 
recovery projects. 
 
5.8.7 With regard to JTFRP–EAP (Jhelum Tavi Flood R ecovery Project), an allocation of 
₹259.25 crores has been provided in BE 2026–27, in line with the projection. The 
Committee observes that the flood recovery project could be affected due to weather-
related constraints, contractual issues and limited  working season. The Committee 
recommends close monitoring of the project so that financial releases are matched with 
timely physical progress. 
 
5.8.8 The allocation under the Disaster Response Fu nd remains at ₹279 crores, consistent 
with previous years. The Committee notes that utili sation under this head is demand-
driven and contingent upon occurrence of notified d isasters. However, considering the 
vulnerability of the Union Territory to floods and other natural calamities, the Committee 
recommends that adequate emphasis be placed on prep aredness, mitigation and resilience-
building measures in addition to relief expenditure. 
 
DISASTER MANAGEMENT 
5.8.9 The Committee notes that a sum of ₹279 crores  has been provided annually towards 
contribution to the Union Territory Disaster Respon se Fund (UTDRF) during the last three 
financial years. The allocation has remained unchan ged from 2022–23 to 2024–25 and has been 
fully utilised in each of these years, as detailed below: 
Table 5.24  
(Amount in ₹ crores)  
Financial Year Amount Utilized 
2022-23 279.00 279.00 
2023-24 279.00 279.00 
2024-25 279.00 279.00 
 

124 
 
5.8.10 The Committee notes that the grant of ₹279 c rores in BE 2026-27 is meant for mitigation 
of unforeseen events and disasters. It has been rep orted that the Union Territory was devastated 
by flash floods in August and September 2025, which  caused substantial loss of property and 
infrastructure. The entire allocation was drawn dow n to meet disaster response requirements. 
The UT has indicated that if any additional amount is required, funds will be sought be sought at 
RE stage or from any other avenue. 
 
Observation/ Recommendation 
 
5.8.11 The Committee observes that the consistent f ull utilisation of the Disaster Response 
Fund over successive years, coupled with the recent flood devastation, underscores the high 
vulnerability of the Union Territory to floods, ear thquakes, landslides and extreme 
weather events. In the Committee’s view, since the UT has indicated that if any additional 
amount is required, the fund will be sought at the RE stage. The Ministry may keep a close 
watch and ensure that required financial support may be provided.  
 
5.8.12 The Committee also sought information on the  steps being taken by the Ministry along 
with the Jammu & Kashmir administration to strength en disaster preparedness, enhance early 
warning systems, reconstruct vulnerable infrastruct ure, ensure safety and livelihood security of 
affected communities, and improve the resilience of  critical connectivity infrastructure such as 
National Highway-44.  
 
5.8.13 The Ministry, in its replies, informed that following steps have been taken to strengthen 
Disaster Preparedness:- 
1.
 In pursuance of the Disaster Management Act, 2005, the first comprehensive State 
Disaster Management Plan (SDMP) for J&K was prepared and notified in 2017.  
2. Post reorganisation, the J&K State Disaster Managem ent Authority was reconstituted as 
the UT-level Disaster Management Authority. The SDM P is since being updated 
continuously.  
3. State Disaster Management Authority and State Execu tive Committee (SEC) have been 
re-constituted. 
4. Directorate of Disaster Management in the Departmen t of DMRRR has been created in 
lieu of existing Office of ERO J&K. 
5. Advisory Committee for Disaster Management and Comm ittee on Landslide Risk 
Mitigation in the UT of J&K has been constituted. 
6. District Disaster Management Plans (DDMPs) have bee n prepared/approved for all 20 
districts (100% district coverage). 
 
Building response capacity: SDRF/NDRF posture, interoperability, training and drills: 
 
1. SDRF strength & deployment footprint (UT J&K): 
 Two SDRF battalions exist with sanctioned strength of 779 posts each. 

125 
 
 SDRF components in all 20 districts: 513 personnel. 
 SDRF sub-components at 13 vulnerable locations: 121 personnel. 
 Quick Response Teams (QRTs) framed at Battalion HQs. 
 01 Company of National Disaster Response Force each  deployed at Srinagar and 
Jammu for joint rescue duties with SDRF. 
 
2.
 Community institutions & volunteer capacity (PRI/community-based preparedness): 
 Aapda Mitra: 2,100 volunteers trained. 
 Yuva Aapda Mitra: 92 trained. 
 Aapda Sakhi: 70 trained. 
 Civil Defence volunteers: 10,660 enrolled/trained; additional target of 14,996 
fixed for training. 
 Institutional mechanisms: Block-level and Panchayat -level Disaster Management 
Committees constituted; Panchayat Ghars proposed/us ed as emergency 
coordination hubs; CSCs envisaged as digital hubs during disasters. 
 
3. Training/awareness/drills and operations (quantified): 
 During 2025: 753 one-day awareness camps, 139 train ing camps, 208 rescue 
operations, and 346 mock drills conducted. 
 
Reconstruction/mitigation of vulnerable infrastruct ure (including flood protection, slope 
stabilisation) - facts & figures:  
1. Road restoration: A total of 2,378 roads were affec ted, and restoration was undertaken 
and stated to be substantially completed to ensure connectivity. 
2. Flood protection works (post 2021–23): Undertaken i n high-risk belts such as Baramulla, 
Uri and Ramban. 
3. Landslide Risk Mitigation Programme: A dedicated al location of ₹15 crores earmarked; 
DPRs from four districts (Doda, Ramban, Kathua - as  recorded) approved by the 
competent authority for execution. 
 
Climate-risk monitoring for high-mountain hazards ( GLOF/snow zones) — institutional 
and quantitative status:  
1. Focused GLOF Monitoring Committee (FGMC) constitute d; subsequently, Core 
Committee for GLOFs constituted. 
2. GLOF risk mitigation programme: Allocation of ₹15 crores for the UT. 
3. Centre for Development of Advanced Computing to act  as Lead Technical Agency and 
prepare DPR. 
 
Enhancing early warning systems and last-mile dissemination:  
1. Common Alerting Protocol (CAP) system adopted for U T-level dissemination of early 
warnings (avalanches, cloudbursts, thunderstorms wi th strong winds, forest fires, 
landslides, etc.), enabling activation of the response mechanism. 

126 
 
2. Multi-agency alert integration: CAP integrated with  alert-generating agencies including 
India Meteorological Department, Central Water Comm ission, DGRE, FSI, INCOIS and 
National Centre for Seismology (NCS)/IIT-linked net works; alerts integrated into UT-
EOC through CAP-based alerts on SACHET, with SOPs f or dissemination to district 
administrations and communities. 
3. Quantified outreach and utilisation (January 2025 to date):  
 154 CAP alerts disseminated. 
 569,387,552 SMS disseminated through CAP (from 01.01.2025 till date). 
 696 ERSS (Dial-112) cases related to various natura l disasters including fire 
received and addressed. 
 509 cases scrutinised and approved on the Decision Support System (DSS) 
platform (for structured documentation/tracking and coordinated action). 
4. Hydro-meteorological monitoring augmentation (I&FC):  
 Kashmir: 22 Automatic Water Level Recorders and 8 A utomatic Rain Gauge 
Recorders installed in the Jhelum basin (hourly transmission). 
 Flood Early Warning System “Kashmir Flood Watch” developed. 
 Jammu: 6 Automatic Water Level Recorders installed in Chenab, Tawi and Ujh 
rivers (real-time transmission). 
 
Strengthening Emergency Operations Centres (EOCs), coordination and decision support:  
The UT-EOC is equipped with ERSS (Dial-112), DSS so ftware, CAP, SACHET, wireless 
communication and satellite communication for real- time incident receipt and response 
initiation. 
 
Winter-cutoff protocol (lifeline & livelihood continuity): 
Advance dumping/pre-positioning of essential suppli es (foodgrains, LPG, kerosene/diesel, 
medicines, fodder/veterinary supplies, etc.) for snow-bound/cut-off pockets (e.g., Gurez, Karnah, 
Machhil, Keran and high-altitude pockets) through d istrict-wise plans led by Deputy 
Commissioners and line departments; monitored via D istrict Emergency Operations Centres 
(DEOCs) and review meetings. 
 
5.8.14 The Committee further enquired about new dis aster preparedness infrastructure, early 
warning capacity and emergency response systems pro posed to be operationalised during 2026–
27, particularly in the context of the continued al location of ₹279 crores towards contribution to 
the Union Territory Disaster Response Fund. The Min istry, in its reply, informed that an 
allocation of ₹279 crores under the Disaster Response Fund (UTDRF) (Central share) is received 
from the Government of India and shall be utilised for disaster management and resilience. The 
Ministry further informed that the following measur es/infrastructure, early warning capacities 
and emergency response systems are being/will be undertaken during 2026–27: 
 
1.
 Multi-hazard vulnerability assessment and mapping for the UT of J&K. 

127 
 
2. Strengthening of early warning and monitoring syste ms, with dissemination up to the last 
mile being the focal area. 
3. Strengthening of institutional mechanisms, includin g updation of the UT Disaster 
Management Plan 2026–27 and District Disaster Manag ement Plans and contingency 
plans. 
4. Completion of the State-of-the-art UT Emergency Operations Centre (UT-EOC). 
5. Augmentation of SDRF with advanced equipment, machinery and other resources. 
6. Capacity-building programmes, training programmes, mock exercises and simulation 
exercises. 
7. Continued training and deployment of community volu nteers, Mitras, Aapda Sakhis and 
local volunteers. 
 
The Ministry also informed that various departments  such as PWD, HUDD and I&FC are 
undertaking projects for mitigation and prevention of disasters or threatening disaster situations 
within their respective domains, with focus on development of disaster-resilient infrastructure. 
 
Observation/ Recommendation 
 
5.8.15 The Committee appreciates the strengthening measures undertaken, including 
reconstitution of the UT-level Disaster Management Authority, preparation and updation 
of the UT Disaster Management Plan and District Dis aster Management Plans, and 
creation of a dedicated Directorate of Disaster Man agement. The Committee also notes the 
strengthening of response capacity through deployme nt of SDRF battalions, positioning of 
NDRF companies, conduct of training camps, mock dri lls and rescue operations, and 
expansion of community-based volunteer networks. Th ese measures indicate improved 
preparedness and structured response mechanisms. 
  
5.8.16 The Committee also notes the adoption of the  Common Alerting Protocol (CAP) 
system, integration with multiple alert-generating agencies, large-scale dissemination of 
SMS alerts, augmentation of hydro-meteorological mo nitoring infrastructure and 
development of the “Kashmir Flood Watch” system. Th e Committee views these steps as 
significant advancements in early warning and last- mile communication. However, it is of 
the view that periodic evaluation of system reliabi lity, alert accuracy and community 
responsiveness must be ensured for effective outcomes. 
 
5.8.17 While acknowledging the restoration of affec ted roads, flood protection works and 
the Landslide Risk Mitigation Programme, the Commit tee observes that mitigation efforts 
must receive sustained and enhanced attention. In p articular, climate-resilient 
strengthening of critical infrastructure such as Na tional Highway-44 and other lifeline 
roads must be prioritised to minimise disruption du ring extreme weather events. The 
Committee recommends that disaster risk reduction b e mainstreamed into planning, 
design and execution of all major infrastructure pr ojects undertaken by departments such 

128 
 
as Public Works Department (PWD), Housing and Urban  Development Department 
(HUDD) and Irrigation and Flood Control Department (I&FC). 
 
5.8.18 The Committee also notes the proposed initia tives for 2026–27, including multi-
hazard vulnerability assessment and mapping, comple tion of the State-of-the-art UT 
Emergency Operations Centre (UT-EOC), augmentation of SDRF with advanced 
equipments and continued capacity-building and simu lation exercises. The Committee 
recommends that these initiatives be implemented in  a time-bound manner with clearly 
defined milestones and outcome indicators. The resu lts of vulnerability mapping exercises 
may be integrated with land-use planning, infrastru cture siting and regulatory 
frameworks. 
 
5.8.19 The Committee underscores the importance of shifting the emphasis progressively 
from relief-centric expenditure to long-term mitiga tion, resilience-building and risk 
reduction. Investment in slope stabilisation, embankment strengthening, glacier and GLOF 
monitoring, floodplain management and community-bas ed preparedness will reduce 
recurring expenditure on restoration of damaged infrastructure and compensation . 
 
TOURISM 
 
5.8.20  The Committee sought to know the present status of tourism in Jammu & Kashmir in 
terms of domestic and foreign tourist arrivals and whether tourism has revived after the 
Pahalgam incident. The Committee further enquired a bout the development of new tourist 
destinations such as Kokernag, Bhaderwah and Doodhp athri for sustainable tourism growth in 
view of the increasing tourist footfall. 
 
5.8.21 The Ministry informed that tourist arrivals have shown consistent growth in recent years. 
The number of tourist visits increased from 1.88 cr ores in 2022 to 2.11 crores in 2023 and 2.36 
crores in 2024, which significantly strengthened li velihood opportunities across the sector. 
Approximately 66,000 foreign tourists visited in 20 24, reflecting enhanced global confidence 
and contributing to higher-value tourism revenue.

5.8.22 The Ministry further informed that despite t he unfortunate Pahalgam incident, the

Administration and stakeholders, confidence among v isitors was gradually restored, leading to a 
consistent increase in tourist arrivals from June 2 025 onwards. By the end of December 2025, 
cumulative tourist footfall reached approximately 1 .78 crores, including more than 36,000 
foreign tourists, reflecting a gradual upward trend.

Department of Tourism is actively engaged in a mult ifaceted approach to develop new and 
emerging tourist destinations across the UT. Destin ations such as Kokernag, Bhaderwah and 
Doodhpathri have been developed under the State Cap ex Budget and the Swadesh Darshan 
Scheme for sustainable tourism development.

Observations /Recommendations  
 
5.8.25  The Committee notes with satisfaction the significa nt growth in tourist arrivals 
from 2022 to 2024, with total footfall increasing f rom 1.88 crores to 2.36 crores. The sharp 
increase in foreign tourist arrivals from about 20, 000 in 2022 to over 65,000 in 2024 is 
particularly noteworthy and reflects growing intern ational confidence in the Union 
Territory as a tourist destination. 

130 
 
 
5.8.26 The Committee further observes that although  there was a decline in tourist 
arrivals in 2025 compared to 2024, largely attributable to the Pahalgam incident, the sector 
demonstrates resilience with recovery visible from June 2025 onwards. The cumulative 
footfall of approximately 1.78 crores by December 2 025 indicates restoration of confidence 
and sustained interest among visitors. 
 
5.8.27 The Committee is of the view that tourism co ntinues to be one of the key drivers of 
economic activity and employment generation in the Union Territory. The upward trend in 
tourist arrivals during recent years has contribute d significantly to livelihoods in 
hospitality, transport, handicrafts and allied sect ors. With regard to development of new 
destinations, the Committee notes the allocation of  ₹26.24 crores under the State Capex 
Budget and ₹20.33 crores under the Swadesh Darshan Scheme for development of 
infrastructure at Kokernag, Bhaderwah, Doodhpathri and adjoining areas. The Committee 
appreciates the efforts made to diversify tourism b eyond traditional hubs such as Srinagar, 
Gulmarg and Pahalgam. 
 
5.8.28 The Committee observes that sustained growth  in tourist footfall necessitates 
comprehensive planning to avoid over-concentration and ecological stress in popular 
destinations. The Committee recommends that develop ment of emerging destinations must 
be accompanied by adequate infrastructure, waste ma nagement systems, carrying capacity 
assessment, connectivity improvement and local comm unity participation. A long-term 
sustainable tourism strategy be prepared integratin g environmental safeguards and 
tourists carrying capacity norms. Infrastructure de velopment initiatives at emerging 
destinations may be expedited in a time-bound manner with clear outcome indicators.  
 
5.8.29 The Committee further recommends greater emp hasis may be placed on high-value 
tourism segments such as eco-tourism, adventure tou rism and winter tourism. Skill 
development and entrepreneurship support may also b e provided to local youth to 
maximise livelihood benefits. Measures for periodic  monitoring of tourist satisfaction, 
safety perception and infrastructure adequacy could be initiated. The Committee reiterates 
that tourism has emerged as a vital sector for econ omic revitalisation of Jammu & 
Kashmir and sustained focus on diversification, inf rastructure strengthening and 
confidence-building measures will be essential to maintain the growth momentum. 
 
Migrant Rehabilitation 
 
5.8.30 The Committee sought detailed information re garding the status of rehabilitation of 
Kashmiri migrants, including the number of register ed migrant families, housing facilities 
provision, employment generation under special pack ages, financial assistance, and measures 
taken to ensure dignified return and sustainable re integration. The Committee also sought 

131 
 
information regarding the implementation status of transit accommodation projects and other 
welfare measures. 
 
5.8.31 The Ministry, in its replies informed that 4 7,466 migrant families are presently registered 
with the office of the Relief and Rehabilitation Co mmissioner (Migrants), Jammu for relief. 
These families are being provided cash relief, free ration and other assistance as per norms. 
 
Scheme-wise Assistance and Progress 
i. Accommodation to Kashmiri Migrants at Jammu  
Initially, Kashmiri Migrants were housed in tented accommodations at different locations in 
Jammu. Subsequently, they have been shifted to 5248  Two Room Tenements (TRTs) 
constructed at various sites in Jammu. All TRT’s ar e allotted to the eligible migrants and are 
presently residing there. 
 
ii. Construction of Transit Accommodation 
Government has also approved construction of 6000 t ransit accommodations at 20 different 
locations across the Kashmir Valley for Kashmiri Mi grant employees (under Prime Minister 
Development Package-2015) engaged/to be engaged in different Departments of Government of 
Jammu and Kashmir in the valley. Out of 6000 Transi t Accommodations, 4112 flats have been 
constructed and work is in progress in respect of remaining 1888 flats. 
 
iii. Cash Assistance 
The cash assistance is being provided to the Migran t Families registered under Relief Category 
@ ₹ 3250/- per month per persons subject to the max imum ceiling of ₹ 13000/- per family per 
month. 
  
iv. Free Ration 
Free ration is being provided at the scale of 9 Kg Rice per person per month, 2 Kg of Atta per 
person per month and 1 Kg of Sugar per family per m onth to the Kashmiri Migrants. In case of 
Jammu Migrants the scale of 9 Kg Atta per person per month, 2 Kg of Rice per person per month 
and 1 Kg of Sugar per family per  month.  
 
v. Employment Package 
In order to facilitate return and rehabilitation of  Kashmiri migrants in the Kashmir valley, during 
2009, Government of India sanctioned 3000 posts und er PMRP–2009 and additional 3,000 posts 
were sanctioned under PMDP–2015. Thus, a total of 6 ,000 posts were earmarked for Kashmiri 
Migrant youth. Against the said posts appointment o rders 5,896 (98.26%) candidates have been 
issued so far and remaining 104 posts are currently under various stages of recruitment.  
 
vi. Issuing of Certificates etc.:  

132 
 
For the convenience of Kashmiri migrants, online se rvices for registration and issuance i.e. 
domicile certificate, issuance of Resident of Backw ard Area (RBA) certificate, EWS certificate, 
income certificate, migrant certificate have been launched.  
 
vii. Immovable Property  
The Government of Jammu & Kashmir has enacted the J ammu & Kashmir Migrant immovable 
property (Preservation, Protection and Restrain on Distress Sales) Act, 1997 which provides for 
the Preservation, Protection and Restrain on Distre ss Sales of Immovable property of Kashmiri 
Migrants. Till date 3408 Kanals and 16 marlas of Ka shmiri migrant land has been retrieved from 
encroachment and illegal occupation. 
 
viii. Other initiatives:  
 Under the PMJAY-Sehat Scheme 63109 cards have been issued.  
 Aadhar Seeding – Direct Benefit Transfer –For transparent and efficient transfer of relief 
(cash assistance), 22051 migrant families have been  Aadhar seeded, which has enabled 
transfer of relief on real time basis efficiently without delay to the real beneficiaries.  
 Outreach programmes& grievance redressal camps have  been launched at various 
locations across the country.  
 Organized socio-cultural events/camps for promotion  of Cultural, language, health, 
sports activities. 
 Educational tours for all the 06 migrant schools ha ve been organized in which more than 
3000 children participated and visited places of historical and tourism importance. 
 Integration of Migrant Ration Cards into NFSA databaseof Smart PDS Portal by GoI. As 
on date, more than 11500 migrant ration cards have been integrated.  
 
ix. Measures for Sustainable Reintegration 
The Government has adopted the following measures: 
 Large-scale employment generation under PM Package. 
 Construction and allotment of transit accommodations. 
 Continuation of cash relief. 
 Educational scholarships. 
 Security and infrastructural support at designated locations. 
 These measures are aimed at ensuring dignified retu rn, livelihood security and 
sustainable reintegration of migrant families in the Kashmir Valley.

Observation/ Recommendation 
 
5.8.33  The Committee notes that 47,466 migrant families co ntinue to be registered for 
relief and that substantial financial and welfare a ssistance is being provided. The 
Committee appreciates the completion and allotment of 5,248 two-room tenements in 
Jammu and the progress made in construction of tran sit accommodations in the Valley. 
However, the Committee observes that while 4,112 tr ansit accommodations have been 
completed, 1,888 units remain under construction. T he Committee recommends that the 
remaining units be completed in a time-bound manner to ensure secure accommodation for 
employees appointed under the special package. 
  
5.8.34 The Committee notes that 5,896 out of 6,000 sanctioned posts have been filled. 
While this represents significant progress, the Com mittee recommends that the remaining 
vacancies be filled expeditiously so that the emplo yment component of the rehabilitation 
package is fully realised. 
 
5.8.35 The Committee appreciates the implementation  of Direct Benefit Transfer (DBT) 
mechanisms, digitisation of documentation processes  and issuance of Pradhan Mantri Jan 
Arogya Yojana PM-JAY SEHAT cards, which enhance tra nsparency and access to 
services.  
 
5.8.36 The Committee recommends that financial reli ef, though necessary may 
progressively be complemented by long-term liveliho od generation, entrepreneurship 
promotion and integration measures. Although, migra nt rehabilitation is not merely a 
welfare measure but also a sensitive socio-economic  and humanitarian issue and sustained 
efforts, adequate housing, secure employment, commu nity confidence-building and 
infrastructure support in areas of return are essen tial for ensuring dignified and 
sustainable reintegration.

Observation/ Recommendation 
5.9.6 The Committee notes that Central Assistance c ontinues to constitute a significant 
component of the resource structure of the Union Te rritory. While the allocation for     
2026-27 maintains parity with RE 2025-26, there exists a substantial gap between projected 
demand and allocated resources, particularly in res pect of infrastructure and externally 
aided projects.  
  
5.9.7 The Committee recommends that the Ministry ma y review the resource position 
during the course of the financial year and conside r additional support at the Revised 
Estimates stage, particularly for infrastructure an d externally aided projects, based on 
progress of implementation. Continued efforts may b e made to strengthen own revenue 
mobilisation, rationalise expenditure and improve f iscal sustainability so that the Union 
Territory may gradually reduce its long-term depend ence on Central Assistance and 
borrowings.
